Irish Government issues research sector security guidelines
2026-09-07

Irish Government issues research sector security guidelines

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
The recommendations ‘do not introduce new obligations, but support institutions in understanding and implementing relevant existing responsibilities in a proportionate manner’. Ireland’s research ecosystem will be subject to new national guidelines on research security to safeguard the sector against intellectual property theft and misuse, foreign interference, and emerging security threats. ‘Research Security Guidelines for Ireland’, created by the Irish Government and published today (7 September), is aiming for alignment with international best practice while protecting innovation and strengthening global research partnerships. The guidelines outline Ireland’s national approach to research security, and how the risks associated with international research and innovation collaboration are identified, assessed and managed in a proportionate and context-sensitive manner, according to the Department of Further and Higher Education, Research, Innovation and Science. The framework is intended as enabling guidance – rather than a regulatory or enforcement document – for Ireland’s research performing organisation (RPOs), funders of research and other stakeholders in the country’s research ecosystem. The guidelines “do not introduce new obligations, but support institutions in understanding and implementing relevant existing responsibilities in a proportionate manner”, according to the document, and are based on key principles of: risk management rather than risk avoidance; proportionate responses based on institutional autonomy; clarity of roles and responsibilities; and avoidance of bureaucratic complexity that stifles innovation. Minister for Further and Higher Education, Research, Innovation and Science James Lawless, TD said: “Ireland’s research system is among the most internationally connected in the world. That openness is one of our greatest strengths but it must be matched by an ability to identify and manage emerging security risks. “These guidelines represent a significant step forward in ensuring that Ireland remains both open and secure as a global leader in research and innovation. “This is about protecting valuable knowledge, supporting our researchers and making sure our research system remains open and trusted. “Many institutions are already doing good work in this area. What these guidelines do is provide a common framework so that research organisations across the country can build on that experience and take a consistent approach.” The guidelines are the product of collaboration between Government departments, research funders and RPOs, and originate from a May 2024 EU recommendation that member states develop national research frameworks with clear roles and responsibilities. RPOs retain autonomy and decision-making on how to implement the new guidelines, according to the Government, which said it will act as a coordinator and support mechanism during the implementation phase of the initiative that is now underway. The guidelines are available to the public on the Government’s website. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Quantum funding only a part of the solution, EU experts say
2026-09-07

Quantum funding only a part of the solution, EU experts say

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Europe’s Quantum Moment: From Lab to Leadership. Image: Quantum Flagship EU Quantum Act policy expected by the end of this year. European policymakers, business personalities and investors gathered at the European Parliament last week for a high-level discussion about the next phase of the bloc’s quantum development. The panel touched on areas surrounding the development and sustenance of quantum technology in the bloc, with speakers noting that the region needs to translate its scientific expertise in the sector into industrial leadership that can be deployed across the single market. The event, called ‘Europe’s Quantum Moment: From Lab to Leadership’, was hosted by EU MEP Dr Sergey Lagodinsky, who, in agreement with other speakers, said that the bloc needs to understand how scale up and bring together scientists, engineers and start-ups to transform opportunities in the quantum sector into commercial capacity comparable to the US, which is already investing hundreds of millions in this type of technology. Between 2020 and 2025, the EU invested some €2bn in quantum technologies, complemented by around €9bn in additional funding from member states. In comparison, the US spent $2bn in federal incentives a few months ago to support two domestic quantum foundry companies and seven quantum computing companies, including IBM. The speakers at last week’s panel noted that Europe already has a strong base to build on, with research groups and companies active across areas including quantum computing, sensing, photonics, cryogenics and software. The bloc also has an established footing in this sector, with around 80pc of the components in quantum computers in Europe come from the bloc, a speaker said. The EU has a number of up and coming quantum businesses, including Ireland’s Equal1, which raised $60m in January, Switzerland-based Zuriq, which raised a seed funding round of $25.5m in July, and Finland’s IQM Quantum Computers, which, earlier this year, became the first European quantum computing company to list on a major US stock exchange. But despite the potential and significant funding from the EU and its member states, Europe’s quantum research remains fragmented across member states, resulting in duplication, gaps in priority areas and competition for scarce talents, according to an EU policy document from last year, which also noted that without coordination and a clear focus on shared strategic priorities, Europe will fall short of its quantum ambitions. “You talk to scientists and they will tell you nothing is ready and a quantum computer is still 25 years away. You talk to some American media, and they say it’s for tomorrow. So, what is the reality then? How do we position ourselves?”, said Thomas Skordas, the deputy director-general of European Commission’s Directorate-General for Communications Networks, Content and Technology. “The reality is that, of course, we still need a lot of work, but we also see a super-emerging quantum ecosystem, not only in computing, but also in communications and in sensors, where some of the more mature markets are found today.” The upcoming Quantum Act is expected to help boost research and innovation, strengthen Europe’s industrial capacity and improve the resilience of the quantum supply chain. The EU has already begun supporting deep-tech companies in the region with its new €5bn Scaleup Europe Fund, which officially launched last month. The €5bn fund has already backed Swedish AI vibe-coding start-up Lovable and Finnish space intelligence start-up Iceye. Budgeting, however, isn’t the only solution, according to Skordas. “What the act will provide is the stimulus to make sure that we move in the right direction, that we bring people together,” he noted. Quantum technologies require levels of capital, fabrication and testing facilities that EU member states alone cannot always provide. Meanwhile, experts also worry that a lack of coordination between states will lead to duplication, unequal access and concentration of quantum capabilities in Europe’s more advanced countries. “We cannot afford having 78 start-ups all on their own”, said Skordas. “We don’t have Microsoft, we don’t have Google, we don’t have IBM… we want competition, but at some stage, we expect a little bit of consolidation”. Skordas stressed that the fragmentation between technologies also needs to be addressed. “Quantum has to be combined with semiconductors, photonics, the cloud industry, the end users and many other technologies that are more mature and can win new markets”. The Commission representative confirmed that the bloc hopes to present the new quantum policy by the end of the year. Although the process is still in its early stages, it might end up being postponed until next year, he added. Talent acquisition was another aspect discussed in the panel last week. “We need software engineers, we need photonics people, we need physics people, we need product managers, we need all sorts of different profiles”, explained Prof Eleni Diamanti, the co-founder and chief scientific officer at the Paris-based quantum company Welinq and CNRS Research Director at Sorbonne University. “We need to make everything we can to bring this talent to Europe. For example, by giving opportunities and raising salaries” she added. “If we create global quantum champions in Europe, which is what I hope that is going to come out of the quantum act, then the talent will come. The talent goes where the global leaders are, not only where the money is.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Lower salaries resulting in weaker training models, finds report
2026-09-04

Lower salaries resulting in weaker training models, finds report

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Iris Software Group’s research explored how more established employees are unwilling to train up less experienced peers who are on a similar salary. Much is expected of employees in 2026, particularly in the wake of a working environment that has been transformed and reimagined multiple times since the introduction of AI and workplace automation. Professionals are often expected to work across a range of teams, to hold a diverse yet specialised skillset and to undertake additional responsibilities, often for a salary that is not reflective of the work being carried out. One such obligation often bestowed upon professionals is duties related to the training and support of younger or less experienced co-workers, often as a means of creating a consistent and in-house system of learning. It also means that if and when a professional moves on from the role, there is an equally qualified person ready to step into the position. This is the focus of new research conducted by Iris Software Group. On behalf of the organisation, Censuswide collected data from 511 UK-based senior HR professionals and 500 UK employees who have been in full-time work for between two and five years. What was discovered is that 71pc of young professionals that have been employed for between two and five years find it difficult to feel motivated to train or help a new starter earning almost as much as them. In scenarios where a young professional discovered what a new starter earns, 24pc said that it was the same as their own salary. Almost 40pc said that it was up to £1,000 less, while only 16pc said it was between £1,000 and £3,000 less. A further 16pc said that they had discovered new starters were being paid more than they were. Commenting on the report, Stephanie Coward, the managing director for HCM at Iris, said: “It’s not surprising to see frustration building when young professionals are taking on harder work, more responsibility and helping train the next intake, but see almost no difference in their pay. “Businesses have faced genuine pressure to raise starting salaries, including increases to the National Minimum Wage and National Living Wage. But this isn’t only being driven by forces outside employers’ control. HR professionals also pointed to internal issues such as salary bands not being reviewed regularly enough.” Think for impact with Liberty IT. Delivering global software solutions Join a culture that offers a world of possibilities Almost a third (32pc) of HR leaders who participated in the research admitted that the gap between young professionals and those who are just starting out has narrowed over the course of the last two years. Among the factors noted by the report as having a part to play in pay compression are rising national minimum and living wages in the UK, as well as companies increasingly prioritising attractive starter salaries over pay rises for the existing staff. The research also indicated that there is little positivity among HR leaders that this pay compression is going to improve. Almost half (46pc) of contributors explained that they expect salary bands to narrow further over the course of the next two years. Among the surveyed young professionals who learned what new starters were being paid in comparison to themselves, 69pc said it had left them feeling undervalued, frustrated or disappointed. Some were motivated to take action, as 16pc accepted a new job offer and a third requested a pay increase. Moreover, despite 84pc of participating HR leaders concerned about the risk of losing the loyalty of young professionals who feel as though their pay does not reflect their additional responsibilities and experience compared to new recruits, only 20pc have plans to make targeted salary adjustments. Coward said: “Compounding the problem, almost a quarter (23pc) of HR leaders said their current HR systems are not capable of comparing pay against changes in employees’ skills and responsibilities, making it harder to identify where pay compression is emerging and which employees are most affected. “Most businesses won’t be able to solve this with blanket pay rises. But they do need to understand where compression is happening and where it risks costing them good people. A targeted salary increase may look expensive, but so is losing an experienced employee, recruiting their replacement and rebuilding the knowledge that walks out of the door with them.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Hubble Space Telescope captures nebula ‘superbubble’
2026-09-04

Hubble Space Telescope captures nebula ‘superbubble’

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
The nebula LHA 120-N44, or N44. Image: NASA/ESA/Hubble/D Gouliermis NASA and the ESA said the vast central void seen above measures around 210 by 140 light-years in area, and was likely created by the stars visible at its centre. NASA and the ESA have published striking images of a nebula ‘superbubble’ in the Dorado constellation that were captured using the Hubble Space Telescope. ‘LHA 120-N44’ – or N44 – is described as “a complex nebula filled with glowing hydrogen gas, dark lanes of dust, massive stars and many populations of stars of different ages”. N44 is part of the Large Magellanic Cloud (LMC) – the largest of the small galaxies that orbit our Milky Way galaxy, according to the space agencies, and about 160,000 light-years away from Earth. The superbubble – the vast central void visible in the image above – measures around 210 by 140 light-years in area, and was likely created by the stars visible in its centre, according to the agencies. N44 spans about 1,000 light-years overall, and is described as a “highly active star birth site”. The stars at the centre of the bubble are thought to have expelled – through “powerful stellar winds and explosive supernovae” – much of the gas from which they were created. This dislodged gas then compressed and formed a dense and dusty ‘shell’ around the bubble, now visible as the colourful perimeter to the central dark void. NASA and the ESA said that new star formation within the nebula’s compressed gas shell is ongoing, and the subject of study by astronomers, who want to figure out the duration between the collapse of cold gas clouds into dense knots and the moment when nuclear fusion ignites in the heart of a newborn star. A possible 5m-year age difference between stars within the superbubble and at its rim was previously identified by NASA. The deep blue area to the bottom-right of the superbubble is one of the hottest regions of the nebula, according to researchers, and the site of the most intense star formation. N44 is an emission nebula, which means its gas has been energised, or ionised, by the radiation of nearby stars. A separate, smaller bubble formation is visible in the top-right of the image – this is known as N44F and is an “interstellar bubble blown by the intense stellar winds of a single hot and massive star”. The image displayed derives from research using Hubble to take a census of N44’s stars, of which around 500,000 have been catalogued – with 30,000 deemed to be “pre-main-sequence stars” that have yet to begin fusing hydrogen into helium in their cores. NASA recently launched a new space telescope, the Nancy Grace Roman. In May, its James Webb telescope was used to detect clear evidence that some supermassive black holes were enormous from the beginning, forming without a stellar collapse phase and without a significantly more massive host galaxy to feed them. In January, Hubble was used to capture images of ‘infant stars’ as part of the Sofia Massive Star Formation Survey. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. As Americans are being hailed as folk heroes for sawing down Flock surveillance cameras across the country, British home appliance company Dyson has come up with a form of video surveillance that we didn’t even know was possible. Yes: the company has launched a new AI-infused electric toothbrush that features a “100k pixel macro lens camera,” allowing users — brushers? — to “see what their dentist sees.” The appropriately named Dyson CameraJet claims to analyze 28 images per second and employ an “AI machine-learning algorithm” to identify and target “interdental gaps.” In case you’re wondering how it can take pictures in the darkness of your mouth, a “stroboscopic light” ensures “precise illumination for optimal camera visibility.” It also hooks up to WiFi and Bluetooth, theoretically opening it up to all kinds of cybersecurity shenanigans if somebody were to identify any vulnerabilities in Dyson’s software. “By combining a camera, machine learning, precision fluid dynamics, advanced brushing technologies, connectivity and WiFi, Dyson CameraJet introduces an entirely new way to keep your mouth healthy,” founder and chief engineer James Dyson boasted in a statement. Of course, all of that sensitive video surveillance footage of the inside of your mouth is going to cost you dearly. The undoubtedly overengineered gizmo — which looks more like an undersized hair curling iron than a familiar electric toothbrush — goes for a whopping $499 and at least comes with two brush heads, a charging cable, and a “refill dock.” Naturally, Dyson will happily sell you a “proprietary non-foaming toothpaste” as well, which lacks a common synthetic foaming agent called sodium lauryl sulfate (SLS) to make sure the toothbrush surveillance camera isn’t impeded by all those suds. We’ll leave it up to you whether you deem the product, which allegedly required the input of 661 engineers, is worth the price of an iPad Air or roughly ten Oral-B Pro 1000 electric toothbrushes — a popular budget pick for those who don’t need AI to brush their teeth. Speaking to the New York Times, Scott Mlakar, chief of the Lake County Drone Unit outside Cleveland, called the tariffs financially onerous and lamented that it would prevent many communities from buying them, adding that his unit uses them to hunt crime suspects at night and search for lost children. “Most of this, in our humble opinion, is lobbying by US manufacturers to eliminate foreign competition,” he said. “This is not national security. Drones have a lot of other practical uses beyond law enforcement, obviously, including monitoring wild fires and assisting rescue operations. New AI Designed to Replace CEOs

New AI Designed to Replace CEOs

Futurism | 2026-09-06

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. A group of software engineers who say they were fired by their bosses because of AI are giving their former employers a taste of their own medicine. With the powers of spite and friendship, they’ve built “OpenExecutive,” an AI system designed to do the work of a CEO, if not the entire c-suite, TechRadar reports. “Work happens around the clock. Leadership doesn’t scale with it,” a demo video begins, as a stock music track called “Corporate Music Uplifting” plays in the background. The team describes their AI system, perhaps with their tongues in their cheeks, as a “senior advisor with Harvard MBA-level knowledge.” Whether you believe that or not probably reflects how much stock you put in Harvard MBAs. In any case, OpenExecutive is built using several of Anthropic’s Claude AI models, the default being Claude Sonnet 4.6, with Claude Opus 4.7 being reserved for “deep reasoning” tasks. It recruits eight AI agents that each act as a different top level executive, including the chief strategy officer, chief financial officer, and the head of human resources. Together, they form a single unified “executive persona” — in effect, the CEO. The developers say that the AI can learn from company documents and remembers its recommendations and decisions — which is more than what you can say for some CEOs who have a conveniently selective memory. If you’re skeptical, you can poke around in the system yourself, since it’s open-source. Is OpenExecutive actually going to outmode the CEO? Probably not, since, whatever your opinions on them, there’s a lot to being the head of a company that a machine can’t emulate, like taking accountability (okay, bad example). But that’s the thing: you could argue the same thing for their employees, with the notable difference that the rank and file are the ones doing almost all the labor. And so, going by CEOs’ own specious logic, now that a tool exists that can do their job more efficiently than they can— coming with none of inconveniences that human workers bring, like going on strike or asking for a raise — then they should be okay with being replaced. Of course, some AI-obsessed bigwigs may see tools like this a boon. Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg is reportedly building a CEO AI agent to help him do his job. He’s also building a photorealistic AI clone of himself so he can be virtually omnipresent throughout the company. Or maybe that’s the lie being fed to him by his underlings building the AI CEOs, so that he’ll be usurped by thinking machines once they become powerful enough. While they’re seemingly still years away from completing actually useful tasks, like cleaning a messy apartment or doing the dishes, humanoid robots have developed plenty of capabilities over the past few years, from sprinting faster than any human ever to performing elaborate dance choreographies. They’ve also been taught how to punch and even kickbox for their human overlords’ amusement, as one man visiting a store in Russia appears to have learned the hard way. Scientists Find That 96 Percent of Delicious Tuna Is Infected With Parasitic Worms
2026-09-06

Scientists Find That 96 Percent of Delicious Tuna Is Infected With Parasitic Worms

Futurism | 2026-09-06

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. For seafood lovers, there are few things more appealing than a simple slab of raw tuna — a fish whose fatty, savory, and tender muscle tissue has been a delicacy for thousands of years. Unfortunately for those foodies, fresh raw fish comes with the distinct possibility of parasitic infection. Off the coasts of southern Brazil, that risk is rising, as hauls of the common skipjack tuna come with the near certainty of parasitic contamination. That’s not an exaggeration. According to a new report published in the journal Ocean and Costal Research and flagged by ScienceAlert, a whopping 96 percent of a recent load of tuna was infested with gut-busting worms. Out of 53 skipjack, marine researchers counted over 1,600 worms infesting nearly every organ of every fish hauled in from the ocean. Only the kidneys and hearts of the tuna remained worm-free, the researchers noted. Everything else, including the muscle tissue we humans eat, was festering with flatworms and roundworms. Though most preparation methods will kill the parasites, the images are pretty off-putting. As the researchers explain, the sheer volume of parasitic infection is alarming. “These results highlight the importance of sanitary inspection and emphasize the importance of immediate evisceration to avoid the migration of larvae from the viscera to the musculature,” the authors write. Still, parasitologists say the worms aren’t all bad. Though increased presence of worms also comes with more risk of infection, University of Washington parasitologist Chelsea Wood told ScienceAlert in a prior story that they can actually be a sign that the fish is well-sourced. “Everyone assumes that worms in your salmon is a sign that things have gone awry,” Wood said. “But the anisakid [roundworm] life cycle integrates many components of the food web. I see their presence as a signal that the fish on your plate came from a healthy ecosystem.” More on food: Scientists Turn Plastic Waste Into Edible Cookies Because That’s the Future We Deserve

It Turns Out That Drivers Absolutely Detest Those Giant Touchscreens They Put in Cars Now
2026-09-06

It Turns Out That Drivers Absolutely Detest Those Giant Touchscreens They Put in Cars Now

Futurism | 2026-09-06

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. Automakers are having an increasingly difficult time convincing their customers that more in-car screens — and the often dodgy software that accompanies them — are a compelling reason to pay a major premium. Virtually every major car company is now installing increasingly enormous screens into the dashboards of its cars, frequently stretching far beyond the driver’s seat and into the passenger area as well. But what’s becoming more and more clear is that practically nobody actually wants any of those purported luxuries in their ride. As a new survey by consumer research firm JD Power goes to show, owners of 2026 model-year vehicles are being actively put off by the enormous displays. Out of the 68,000 owners JD Power surveyed, over a third of them said that they never even tried to use passenger screens, while only six percent said they use them every time they get in the car. Worse yet, in its own investigation, JD Power found that the larger screens were more prone to infotainment system problems as well. The survey made clear that the most popular consumer-facing car technologies, out of the 40 examined, were the ones that were the most easily ignored, such as smart ignition or smart HVAC. The lowest on the entire list, tellingly, were passenger console touchscreens — which have previously been linked to severe driving safety issues that were absent for traditional knobs and buttons. “The technology customers value most is often the technology they barely notice because it simply works,” said JD Power director of customer success Doron Hikry in an accompanying press release. “Smart ignition and smart climate control make everyday driving easier without demanding the driver’s attention, while more automation and additional screens don’t necessarily translate into a better experience.” Beyond wasted space for giant screens, owners also weren’t impressed with Level 2 Plus driver assistance systems, which allow them to drive without having to keep their hands on the wheel on certain roads, such as Tesla’s infamous Full Self-Driving feature. In its survey, JD Power found that owners preferred Level 2 systems in terms of both design and quality of experience. “The results indicate that owners may currently be more comfortable with systems that clearly assist the driver rather than those that introduce a greater degree of automation,” Hikry added. It’s an already familiar trend, with consumers growing frustrated with every feature getting buried in convoluted touchscreen interfaces. Some automakers have caught on to the widespread frustration, opting to fall back on easier-to-use and less distracting tactile controls for car interiors. Authors push back as publishers and agents make claims on Anthropic settlement
2026-09-06

Authors push back as publishers and agents make claims on Anthropic settlement

TechCrunch | 2026-09-06

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Some authors hoping to receive their share of Anthropic’s $1.5 billion copyright settlement said they received surprising emails this week — emails informing them that someone else was making a claim on their payments. Anthropic settled a copyright class action suit last year, after a judge ruled that training AI models on copyrighted material is legal under fair use doctrine, but pirating that material was not. The deal received final approval in July, allowing the payments to move forward. Under the terms of the settlement, the authors of nearly 500,000 titles will be paid $3,000 for each pirated work. If the book is still in-print with a traditional publisher, the money will be split 50-50 between author and publisher. If the book was self-published, or if the publisher reverted the rights by allowing the book to go out-of-print, then the author should get the entire payment. But writers have been posting on social media that publishers seem to be claiming more than their fair share of some payments. For example, mystery and thriller author April Henry asked, “WTF is HarperCollins playing at? They claimed one of my books on the Anthropic Settlement that reverted back at least 17 years ago AND on the same day I got a credit alert saying they had been added as my employer! (which they never were).” At the popular blog Writers Beware, Victoria Strauss wrote that she’s been receiving author complaints that fall into two broad categories: one where publishers are seeking payment for works that they no longer have a legitimate claim on (because the rights have reverted), and another where publishers are seeking a full 100% payment when they’re only entitled to 50%. In both cases, Strauss said she’s “reluctant to attribute to malice what can be plausibly explained by poor recordkeeping” — and she noted that some publishers have already said this is a mistake that they’ve asked Anthropic to fix. Similarly, Authors Guild CEO Mary Rasenberger told The New York Times that she doesn’t see this as “a grab by the publishers” and that she doesn’t believe publishers are “specifically trying to screw any author over.” Instead, she argued that this is the predictable result of bad record-keeping and a confusing settlement process. Strauss also acknowledged that any complaints she’s seen are just “a peek through a small crack in a massive wall.” “But the unusually large number of reports I’ve received over the last two days, as well as the fact that authors are reporting the exact same errors over and over, suggest to me that these aren’t the kind of routine glitches you might expect from such a large operation, but something much more wide[s]pread and systemic,” she wrote. And publishers aren’t the only ones seeking a cut of the payments. Strauss said she’s gotten complaints that a number of literary agencies are also making claims, which she said is surprising since “agents are not rightsholders in the books that they sell.” Author Courtney Milan (the pen name of former law clerk and law professor Heidi Bond) was more blunt in a post on Bluesky, writing, “Apparently some agents are trying to claim percentages on the Anthropic settlement, and I do not REMOTELY think they should do this, what the fuck, stop that shit!” Milan and the Authors Guild also shared more details about how authors can dispute their payment allocations. (One tricky issue: When the rights to a specific book reverted. In order for an author to make a 100% claim on a book, the rights reversion needs to have happened before August 10, 2022, which is the “download date” in the settlement.) Depending on which corner of social media you frequent, the Tesla Cybercab event was either proof that the company is a robotaxi leader delivering the future or an overhyped affair with little substance. Whatever your view, Wall Street wasn’t impressed by the Cybercab event in Austin, which was unusually quiet and lacked the festival-like atmosphere of past Tesla events. CEO Elon Musk didn’t even attend — a notable and puzzling absence, especially considering he has spent years arguing that Tesla’s future — and its value — hinges on robotics and AI. As senior reporter Sean O’Kane wrote before the event, “The Cybercab is Tesla’s fork in the road. This is either the moment the company begins to transform urban transportation as we know it, or the moment that reveals that, despite its many efforts, Tesla really is just an automaker after all.” The event did little to boost confidence, but I don’t put much stock in events or demos. The real test is whether Tesla can launch a robotaxi service safely and at scale. For now, there is no scale, at least compared with Waymo. (As of today, Tesla has registered 45 Cybercabs in Texas, according to the state’s automated vehicle tracker.) Another wrinkle emerged early Friday morning. The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration opened an investigation into Tesla just hours after the company put its first Cybercabs, which lack a steering wheel and pedals, on Austin streets. Federal vehicle safety regulations require manual controls like brake pedals, though the Department of Transportation recently proposed removing those requirements for vehicles that are designed to drive themselves. Tesla chose to self-certify, the standard route taken by automakers for traditional human-driven vehicles. The NHTSA responded by tapping the brakes, although Tesla may choose to operate even as the investigation plays out. To be clear, there is precedent for the NHTSA’s action, and you can read all about it here. Curious about the Cybercab? Newlight, a Bay Area startup developing a hydrogen injection system to make cargo ships more efficient, raised a $9 million seed round that included investment from lomarlabs, the venture arm of Lomar Shipping; BIRD Energy, a joint venture between the U.S. Department of Energy and the Israel Ministry of Energy; deep tech funds Undeterred Capital and CiRi Ventures; and Fusion VC, an accelerator for Israeli startups in the United States. May Mobility’s self-driving system will be integrated into the vehicles. Importantly, it delivers more horsepower and torque than its non-hybrid equivalent while improving fuel economy to 31 mpg (in the combined cycle). Kia tweaked the design of the new Telluride, and it did give me Kia EV9 vibes, although it felt more like a cousin to that EV, not its twin. A few of my positive impressions: It’s roomier than its predecessor and it is quiet on the road. It’s not the fastest three-row SUV out there and some critics have worried that Kia’s move from a V6 to a 4-cylinder engine would sap its power. There is one design choice that I will probably never get over, even though I am sure I would eventually get used to it — and that’s the location of the gear shifter, which is a stalk attached to the right side of the steering column. TIME
How to Read the Room
2026-09-07

How to Read the Room

TIME | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Every room has a parallel conversation running underneath what appears on the surface. It’s a pulse, a vibe, a temperature. This undercurrent of unspoken behavior delivers data instantly, but only if you can decipher it. Neuroscientists, psychologists, anthropologists, and linguists have studied subtle behaviors for years in a quest to unravel the mystery of how humans create meaning together. I’ve spent the last decade training others how to spot invisible signals, detect who holds power, identify which relationship will influence a decision, spot who has already made up their mind, and decipher who is lying. These are the very skills you need to read the room—and they are all learnable. Here are four science-based approaches to improve your ability to do this: Your mood influences your judgment, and cognitive load distorts what you see. Happy people tend to predict positive outcomes and underestimate negative risks. Stressed minds can find hostility in neutral interactions, according to research by Australian psychologist Joseph Forgas. Landmark work by J.A. Easterbrook showed that emotion and strain reduce the range of cues we can take in and process. Overload your mind, and you will misread the room. Heightened cognitive load, the baggage you carry into a meeting or conversation, distorts what you see. When your mind is cluttered with thoughts of a fight with your spouse or an unfinished project, you stop observing what’s happening and start reading others through the prism of your own stress. In this mindset, a flat “thanks” can become a slight, and a colleague’s distraction can feel like contempt. When a room feels like it’s turning against you, the first question worth asking is whether the room changed or your bandwidth did. Every gathering has a vibe. While that may sound like astrology, science tells us it’s brain circuitry, and that we probably catch each other’s moods far more often than we think. Yale neuroscientists led by Joy Hirsch paired strangers face-to-face and used brain imaging to track what transferred when one person watched emotionally charged videos while the other person watched only the video-watcher. The second person began to mirror the video-watcher’s emotional state, even though they never saw the videos themself. A 2023 study in Communications Biology timed the process: strangers watching television together synchronized their emotional responses within 850 milliseconds. The synchronization ran strongest during positive moments, suggesting joy travels freely while we keep our darker feelings hidden. The emotional state you bring into a room can also shape how everyone else behaves before anyone has consciously registered that you’re anxious or angry. An anxious person can make people rush or interrupt others more frequently. A contemptuous person can license cruelty and make dismissive jokes or harsh judgment feel acceptable. A calm person can reduce the temperature long enough for rational thought to return. The message? Manage your own state of mind before you attempt to manage anyone else’s. A group’s intelligence is not the sum of its IQs. Research by Carnegie Mellon Professor Anita Williams Woolley into the collective intelligence of teams found that group performance had less to do with average or maximum individual IQ than with collaboration. She found that strong groups have good communication skills, open minds, and an equal flow of ideas among participants. A room where one person dominates may feel decisive, but it may be losing intelligence in real time. Watch who grabs attention and who recedes into the background. Who gets interrupted? Who is allowed to finish? Who tried to get into the conversation and then gave up? The smartest thing to do, for the group, is to allow everyone to speak and to be heard. Amateur room readers imbue simple gestures with definitive meaning: crossed arms signal defensiveness, eye contact suggests honesty. The pros observe changes from baseline and look for clusters of behavior that point in the same direction. Science tells us how badly shortcuts deceive us. For example, many people believe that liars avoid eye contact. Researchers at an international airport asked 338 passengers from 81 countries to tell the truth or lie about their travel plans on camera. The liars held eye contact longer—deliberately, a beat past comfortable. Meanwhile, honest people, nervous under questioning, glanced away and looked guilty. Fidgeting is also meaningless as a single gesture. Let’s say someone taps their foot in a meeting or plays with their pen nervously. Maybe that’s their norm. The behavior only becomes a signal when their pattern shifts. The savvy room reader asks what triggered the shift from baseline and watches for a cluster of behaviors. Did a particular question cause them to shift their posture, stop their normal foot-tapping, and freeze their blink-rate? That’s worth noting. The question is never, “What does this gesture mean?” The better question is, “What does this gesture mean here, from this person, under these conditions?” Room reading is a powerful skill that’s part social observation and part emotional intelligence. As you start to see people and interactions more clearly, you’ll alter your own behavior accordingly. And when you do, many more doors will open because you won’t be guessing what the moment requires. Update your preferences in Account Settings Ulrich Siegmund, leader of the Alternative for Germany (AfD) party in the eastern state of Saxony-Anhalt, speaks on stage with AfD co-leader Alice Weidel at the party headquarters following the Saxony-Anhalt regional elections in Magdeburg, Germany, on Sept. 6, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Germany’s Alternative for Germany (AfD) is poised to finish ahead of rivals in a state election, coming closer than any other far-right party in the country to governing at a regional level since World War II. The party won 44% of the vote in Saxony-Anhalt’s state election on Sunday, according to provisional results. By comparison, center-right CDU, the party of Chancellor Friedrich Merz, won less than 20%. The state of 2.1 million people saw a record voter turnout of 77%. But the party faces an uncertain path towards power. Without an absolute majority, it would need a coalition partner or support from lawmakers outside the party to form a government—something that German mainstream parties have thus far refused to provide. Nevertheless, the surge in support for the AfD, which ran on a platform of restricting immigration, ending aid to Ukraine, and taking Germany out of the euro, is remarkable in a country where the legacy of Nazism has long kept the far right at the political margins. The party’s Saxony-Anhalt branch is classified by the state’s domestic intelligence as “right-wing extremist,” a designation that its leaders reject. “We have made history in this country,” Ulrich Siegmund, AfD’s lead candidate in Saxony-Anhalt, said after polls closed. “We’re taking our country back, it starts in Saxony-Anhalt,” he added. “A jolt is going through all of Germany.” The result has already drawn global reactions. U.S. President Donald Trump posted a screenshot of the projected results without comment on Truth Social. Members of Trump’s Administration, including Vice President J.D. Vance, previously expressed public support for the party. Tech billionaire Elon Musk, who spoke at AfD’s campaign launch last year, congratulated AfD co-leader Alice Weidel on the projected win. In response, Siegmund thanked him “for your support” and said he would “welcome the opportunity for strong and constructive cooperation.” Russian presidential envoy Kirill Dmitriev posted a screenshot of Trump’s post. He said in the caption,“Historic win for the pragmatic AfD that will put migration under control. The ruling Bidenthink coalition eviscerated,” referring to former U.S. President Joe Biden, a Democrat. The AfD is friendly to the Kremlin and has advocated for Germany to end support for Ukraine. Invoking the collapse of the communist regime in East Germany and the fall of the Berlin Wall, Herbert Kickl, leader of the right-wing Freedom Party of Austria (FPO) said, “As of today, the strong wind of freedom from 1989 is blowing through Germany once again.” Other European leaders expressed alarm over the result. France’s Europe minister, Benjamin Haddad, said the win was a “grave moment in Europe.” Polish Prime Minister Donald Tusk said more bluntly, “In Poland, only idiots or traitors can rejoice at the triumph of the AfD in Germany.” The AfD’s platform in Saxony-Anhalt ranges from tighter immigration controls to a sweeping overhaul of the state’s education system. Among its more controversial proposals is a policy of “remigration,” which is widely interpreted as mass deportation, although the party says it will prioritize deporting people with a criminal record. It has also called for segregating children of refugees and asylum-seekers in schools, and ending war refugee status for Ukrainians fleeing the war with Russia. The party would largely be unable to enact many of its immigration policies in any individual state even if it can form a government, since many of its proposals would require federal action. But it could tighten policing and pursue changes to schools and cultural policy at the state level. The party wants to steer schools and cultural institutions towards more traditional and nationalist values. It has also advocated revising history curricula to shift emphasis away from the Third Reich and for schools to ban rainbow flags. Instead, it wants schools to fly the German national flag daily, sing the national anthem at school celebrations, and teach more Russian language. The AfD has also attacked Bauhaus, calling the influential school and design movement an “aberration of modernism.” Critics, including Germany’s culture minister, have said the AfD’s rhetoric echoes that of the Nazis, who forced the Bauhaus school to close in 1933. Other proposals include establishing citizen patrols and imposing a moratorium on new wind farms. The AfD capitalized on disillusionment with the political establishment and drew on regional and national pride, including “Ostalgie” or nostalgia for aspects of life in the former communist East Germany. That message found fertile ground in eastern strongholds, like Saxony-Anhalt, where economic disparities with western Germany persist decades after reunification. During the campaign, Siegmund frequently rode a blue Simson, a moped manufactured in the former East Germany, a move that drew criticism from the descendants of the Jewish family that founded Simson. The Halle Institute for Economic Research in Germany has warned that the AfD’s anti-immigration policies could deepen Saxony-Anhalt’s labor shortage and cost the state billions in economic output. Merz has also argued that an AfD victory could turn away foreign investment from the state. Founded in 2013, support for the AfD has grown significantly in recent years. The AfD doubled its national vote share in 2025, and is the country’s most popular party nationally, polling at 28%, according to a recent INSA poll. At the same time, support for the federal government led by Merz, which is struggling to revive a sluggish economy, has fallen to historic lows. In the same INSA poll, Merz’s conservative party had 20% support. In Saxony-Anhalt, 87% of voters were dissatisfied with the federal government, according to an ARD poll. Siegmund has said he wants to become state premier and eventually bring the AfD to victory in the 2029 national elections. While the election delivered the AfD a commanding victory, the party still lacks enough votes to govern alone. With 39 seats in the 83-member legislature, the party remains three short of an outright majority, and faces no obvious path to obtaining it. Germany’s established parties have refused to work with the AfD, continuing their “firewall” against cooperating with the far-right. State premier Sven Schulze acknowledged defeat but his party, the CDU, said it would explore talks with other parties about forming a multiparty coalition. The AfD could find unlikely support in the populist Sahra Wagenknecht Alliance, or BSW, which scraped into parliament with 5.3% of the vote. BSW has rejected the firewall and expressed a willingness to speak with the AfD, although it said it would not elect either Siegmund or Schulze as premier. Siegmund has thus far ruled out leading a minority government or adopting an informal arrangement that would leave his government depending on unsteady support. “If necessary there’ll just be new elections, then we’d just get 50 or 55%,” he told ZDF on election night. He said, however, that he would be willing to work with individual lawmakers or parliamentary groups. Thousands joined an anti-AfD demonstration on Saturday in the regional capital of Magdeburg. Some carried banners that read, “life is better without Nazis.” One protester, Laura Saalfeld, told AFP, “Whatever the results, the atmosphere will change. Far-right sympathisers will show themselves more openly on the streets.”

What to Know About the Miami Plane Crash That Killed 5 People

What to Know About the Miami Plane Crash That Killed 5 People

TIME | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings An Amazon cargo plane overshot the runway at Miami International Airport in Florida, killing at least five people, on Sept. 6, 2026, . Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings At least five people were killed and five others were injured after an Amazon cargo plane overshot the runway upon landing at Miami International Airport on Sunday afternoon. Miami-Dade County officials said in an evening press conference that 21 Air Flight 7598, a Boeing 767-300 freighter operating on behalf of Amazon, crashed into several vehicles shortly before 2 p.m. The National Transportation Safety Board and the Federal Aviation Administration said they are investigating the incident. "The airplane, which was operated by 21 Air, was attempting to land when this incident occurred,” Amazon spokesperson Kelly Nantel said in a statement. “We’re working closely with local authorities and officials to learn more and will cooperate fully with any investigation.” Nantel said the company’s “deepest sympathies go out to the families, loved ones, and all those affected by this devastating loss.” The incident has impacted operations in “America’s busiest airport for international freight and second busiest for international passengers,” according to the Miami airport’s website. Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy posted on social media that travelers should anticipate significant delays and potential flight cancellations. A full ground stop had earlier been issued but was lifted about three hours later. Authorities said ground delays are expected until early Monday. According to the FAA, Flight 7598, a Boeing 767-300 cargo aircraft, departed from Luis Muñoz Marín International Airport in San Juan, Puerto Rico. Miami-Dade Sheriff Rosie Cordero-Stutz said during the news conference that the crash happened at around 1:58 p.m. Data from tracking site Flightradar24 shows that the plane had a speed of nearly 130 mph when it left the usable portion of the diagonal runway. It’s unclear what caused the plane to overshoot, and the NTSB and FAA are investigating. The airport’s runways and taxiways were closed, and the aircraft was left disabled at the northwest end of the airport. Some of the runways were later reopened. According to Miami-Dade Fire Rescue, more than 60 units and approximately 200 personnel responded to the scene, arriving at the airport “to find an airplane that had stopped beyond the airport operational boundary with heavy flames and smoke showing.” Photos and videos from the scene showed black smoke billowing from the plane. Miami-Dade Fire Rescue chief Raied Jadallah said in the news conference that several people were trapped in their vehicles. “What we found was a very complex situation here,” Jadallah said, and added that responders also conducted search and rescue of the pilot and co-pilot who were trapped in the aircraft. Jadallah said there were a total of 10 people involved in the crash: five were killed, three are in critical condition and were transported to the trauma center, and another two were transported to a local hospital. It was not clear on Sunday evening whether those killed and injured were on the plane or on the ground. North Carolina-based cargo airline 21 Air began operating Boeing 767-300 freighters on behalf of Amazon in 2024. The company was founded in 2014. Keith Winters, the airline’s CEO, said in a statement on their website that they were “devastated by the accident” and that the company’s “deepest condolences are with the families and loved ones of those who lost their lives.” Flightradar24 data shows the plane involved in the crash was a 32-year-old Boeing 767. The aircraft model is used for both passenger travel and freight delivery. The first Boeing 767 flew on Sept. 26, 1981, and started commercial operations in 1982 with United Airlines. Amazon Air’s cargo network oversees more than 100 aircraft and over 250 flights daily. It contracts all of its cargo flying to other operators. In its fleet are Boeing 737 and 767 planes, as well as the Airbus A330. Citing details from aviation analytics firm Cirium, the New York Times reported that 21 Air runs eight planes for Amazon Air, while Air Transport International operates about 40 planes, and low-cost carrier Sun Country Airlines operates 22. It’s not the first plane crash involving Amazon Air. In February 2019, Atlas Air Flight 3591, a cargo plane flying from Miami to Houston, crashed into Texas’s Trinity Bay. All three people onboard were killed, and a subsequent federal investigation attributed the crash to pilot error. Sunday’s incident is the latest high-profile crash involving a cargo aircraft in the U.S. Last November, more than a dozen people were killed after a UPS plane crashed into an industrial area in Louisville, Ky. shortly after departing Louisville Muhammad Ali International Airport.

Robert Pattinson Is Disturbing and Alluring in Primetime, a Sordid Movie About a Sordid TV Show

Robert Pattinson Is Disturbing and Alluring in Primetime, a Sordid Movie About a Sordid TV Show

TIME | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In the early 2000s, if your idea of a cheap thrill was to see presumed sicko pedophiles caught on camera as they advance upon their ostensibly underage prey, NBC’s To Catch a Predator was your show. The host, Chris Hansen, had once been considered a serious journalist, spearheading investigative reports on events like the Columbine shootings and the World Trade Center attacks. But on the sleazy, sensationalistic To Catch a Predator, he was more of a vigilante showman. The show worked like this: A crusading organization called Perverted Justice would initiate conversations in online chatrooms designed to attract potential sex offenders. Then they’d be lured to sting houses outfitted with hidden cameras, where Hansen and his crew would await their arrival, ready to enact a pruriently dramatic Gotcha! moment, generally followed by an arrest. Nobody likes a pedophile, and without leaving the Barcalounger, viewers could revel in the self-congratulatory certainty that they too were helping to keep the world safe for the kiddos. Documentary filmmaker Lance Oppenheim’s first fiction feature Primetime—premiering here at the Venice Film Festival—fills in some background pixels on the show’s rise and fall, focusing particularly on an ambitious, egotistical, and at least somewhat delusional Hansen, played by Robert Pattinson. It’s 2006, and Hansen and his beleaguered producer Andie (Merritt Wever, bringing an edgy nervousness to the role of a woman struggling to stay afloat in the ruthless world of TV news) get a chance to move their show into a primetime slot. The stakes are high, but the potential rewards are great. Hansen’s hubris balloons, and he infects others with his own inflated sense of the public service he claims to be providing. To complete its mission of reeling in the bad guys, the show employs youthful-looking decoys posing as underage kids. One of these is an elflike performer named Del (singer-songwriter Phoebe Bridgers) who can impersonate either a boy or a girl; she passes some tips along to a nervous newcomer who will come to be known as Decoy Dan (Skyler Gisondo, in a performance laced with touching eagerness). Though he hopes to become a real actor and thinks this is his way in, he wants to quit when he sees how demeaning the gig actually is. But Hansen, with his crocodile smile and purring, almost sultry intonation, persuades him to stick with it, assuring him that he’ll be helping to cleanse the world of evil. Hansen has gotten high on what he perceives as his own power, and his ambitions grow even greater as he and his team try to draw in a “whale,” ideally a major government figure, rather than the pathetically sad—though still dangerous and repellent—lowlifes who tend to creep into their trap. These thankless roles are heroically played by an assortment of actors including Tony Revolori; despite his character’s unsavory and illegal intentions, his tremulous shock at being caught underscores the show’s legally questionable entrapment methods. Skyler Gisondo in Primetime Courtesy of A24 Hansen’s unfolding mania is one of the chief features of Primetime, and Pattinson’s performance is one of the only reasons to see it. As he practices dialogue that he’ll use in the show, drawn from the chatroom fodder that has lured the show’s marks into its trap, he relishes the prurient material a little too much: “I will undress you, you will undress me.” His theatrically precise diction is both disturbing and weirdly alluring. Still, it’s not exactly clear what Primetime is aiming for: it seems to be making the point that while sexual predators are unequivocally bad, the people who make trash TV—particularly a sordid show that’s masquerading as a means of protecting kids and meting out justice—are also immoral, scheming, and just all-around unpleasant. That’s a shaky equivalence. Oppenheim, director of docs like Spermworld and Some Kind of Heaven, structures the film as if he were shaping a mosaic from disorienting shards; sometimes the scenes feel jaggedly disconnected. He and cinematographer David Bolen have given the picture a grubby, grainy look, including sections that mimic early-2000s-style video recording. If you feel dirty watching it, that’s by design—and if you enjoy feeling dirty, that’s your choice.

What the Pentagon’s Sweeping Polygraph Hunt Has Revealed
2026-09-06

What the Pentagon’s Sweeping Polygraph Hunt Has Revealed

TIME | 2026-09-06

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings U.S. Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth waits before welcoming Australian Deputy Prime Minister and Minister of Defense Richard Marles to the Pentagon September 2, 2026 in Arlington, Virginia Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Pentagon investigators administered polygraph tests to dozens of military officers and civilian employees in August as part of an investigation into disclosures about the nation’s dwindling weapons stockpiles. The polygraph tests, first reported by The New York Times last week, followed Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth’s announcement in July that the Pentagon was forming a joint task force with the Department of Justice to identify and prosecute what he called the “unauthorized disclosure ​of sensitive” information to the press. Read More: Trump Vows to Jail ‘Leakers’ Over Reports of Munitions Shortage Amid Iran Talks Spokesman Sean Parnell said in a statement to TIME that the Pentagon does not comment on investigative matters but “takes all leaks of classified national security information extremely seriously and investigates accordingly.” “Securing classified information is critical to ensuring the security of the United States and of our troops deployed around the world,” the statement said. The August interrogations included some 50 members of the Joint Staff, according to the Times. CBS News also reported that several dozen officials were tested earlier this summer, including those from the U.S. Central Command. Sources familiar with the matter told multiple outlets this summer that U.S. supplies of certain critical munitions—including long-range precision-guided missiles and air-defense interceptors—had fallen dangerously low. They suggested that its inventory would likely dwindle further amid the ongoing war with Iran. Sources told The Washington Post that Trump clashed with Hegseth over the matter during a visit to Camp David on July 31, claiming that he had been “misled on extreme munitions shortages that now threaten to limit military options with Iran.” Trump refuted the idea that there were any shortages in a Truth Social post on Aug. 6. “The U.S. has massive amounts of ‘munitions,’ especially of certain types,” Trump wrote in his post. “Additionally, large amounts are being manufactured and shipped to the U.S. as needed.” The President called for lengthy imprisonment for those caught leaking information to the press. “The ‘leakers’ of these treasonous statements are being hunted down,” he added. “Long term jail sentences will be sought!” In a separate post that same day, he denied that he’d fought with Hegseth, calling the reports “completely unfounded rumors.” “I am extremely happy with the job that Pete Hegseth is doing,” he said. Trump intensified his attacks on the coverage on Sept. 3, calling media outlets who reported on the shortages “treasonous scum” who refuse to “accurately report” on the conflict with Iran. Despite Trump’s repeated denials of a broad munitions shortage, the Center for Strategic and International Studies (CSIS) estimated in late July that missile interceptor inventories have dropped—with Patriot inventories now under 1,000 and THAAD inventories at about 250. Exact figures about the U.S. weapons cache are classified—and that’s for a good reason, according to Harrison Mann, a former defense intelligence official and a senior fellow with the advocacy group Win Without War. “That is valuable information to any U.S. adversary,” he tells TIME. “Obviously, if they know that we're out of ammo in some significant way, it changes deterrence calculations for U.S. adversaries.” At the same time, however, he argues that any shortage would likely be clear to the public and to Iran. “Smart people can put together pretty accurate estimates of what the U.S. has used up between looking at what's been produced, which isn't necessarily a closely guarded secret, and looking at what has been expended on the battlefield,” he says. “Maybe not with the same amount of fidelity [as Pentagon officials], but the Iranians can tell when they're not getting hit by as many standoff munitions, and certainly everyone can tell when U.S. and partners have run out of interceptors.” Mark Cancian, a senior adviser with the CSIS Defense and Security Department, says he and his colleague created CSIS’s July report on dwindling munitions using only “unclassified” sources. “If two people at a think tank can do this analysis with entirely unclassified sources, then the Chinese, with tens of thousands of intelligence operatives, and way better sources of information can get much better numbers,” Cancian says. The Pentagon adheres to specific guidelines and restrictions when administering polygraph tests. They are most commonly used in pre-employment and vetting for intelligence agencies like the National Security Agency and the Defense Intelligence Agency. They are also administered when personnel are seeking or maintaining access to top-secret, special-access, or other sensitive information—as well as in the event of unauthorized disclosures of classified information. Pentagon rules require an examinee’s written consent and allow the person to end the examination at any time. For an issue-specific counterintelligence examination, investigators must have interviewed the person, reasonably believe that the person knew about or was involved in the matter, pursue other investigative avenues as thoroughly as circumstances permit, and determine that the polygraph is essential to the investigation. The Defense Secretary is also required to establish a process for monitoring the “responsible and effective” use of polygraphs and to make information about their use available to congressional defense committees. The Pentagon has not released enough information to determine whether investigators followed all those requirements. It also has not disclosed the investigation’s findings. CBS News, however, reported that no one had failed the questions meant to determine whether any officials had leaked information to the media. It also said that the polygraph tests included a wide range of questions pertaining to national security. Specifically, The New York Times reported that officials were asked if they had disclosed classified information to members of the press, according to information provided by its sources close to the investigation, and if they had provided information about diminished munitions stocks. People familiar with the effort told the Times that the tests appeared intended not only to identify a leaker but also to deter officials who might leak classified information to the media in the future. While the use of polygraphs is not unheard of, the sheer number of people targeted during this investigation is uncommon, officials told CBS News. David Pozen, a law professor at Columbia University, pointed TIME toward his article for the Harvard Law Review that assesses information leaks within the federal government. “Periodically, task forces have recommended that the White House take concerted steps to facilitate leak investigations, such as regularizing the use of polygraphs and requiring government employees who speak with the press to provide advance notice, followed by summaries of what was discussed,” he wrote. This was notably implemented during Ronald Reagan’s presidency in 1983. Reagan authorized polygraph testing in investigations of unauthorized classified disclosures, although he barred agencies from using the results alone to discipline employees. Polygraphs have been considered or used in leak investigations under more recent administrations, as well. During the George W. Bush administration, the FBI interviewed nearly 100 members and staff of congressional intelligence committees while investigating leaked intelligence connected to the Sept. 11 attacks. Agents commonly asked whether they would take polygraphs, but most declined. In subsequent years, publicly documented operations involving dozens of senior military officials appear exceedingly rare. But the Trump Administration has used polygraphs in previous leak investigations. In early 2025, the Department of Homeland Security administered lie detector tests to identify people suspected of disclosing plans for immigration raids. That year, members of Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth’s office were reportedly polygraphed in response to a leak investigation. At the direction of Director Kash Patel, the FBI also began using polygraphs in leak investigations in 2025. In a separate episode this year, more than two dozen current and former members of Patel’s security detail—in addition to some information-technology employees—were reportedly tested in an effort to identify sources of leaks about his leadership. Still, Cancian says that polygraph use is more common at intelligence agencies than among senior military staff. He characterizes a polygraph hunt of this scale as incredibly unusual. While no leakers have been identified to the public, and it remains unknown whether the efforts uncovered any, the tests may serve a secondary purpose—which is to dissuade officials from sharing information in the future. Cancian says the investigation stems from an “understandable” desire for senior Pentagon officials to have candid conversations with their employees without worrying about leaks to the press. “Every administration is concerned about that. I think this one is particularly concerned about that,” Cancian says. Otherwise, officers may hesitate to put concerns in writing, circulate formal objections, or talk openly within authorized channels. The Joint Staff is tasked with giving civilian leaders candid assessments of readiness, risk, and operational sustainability, while coordinating with combatant commands around the world. “Meetings get smaller and smaller, fewer and fewer people can attend because of concerns that larger groups, you know, might leak,” he says. Mann adds that a polygraph test is known as an “inconclusive” tool. “You can game them. You can get false positives,” he says. “So, you certainly won't necessarily get to the bottom of whatever you're searching for by running a lot of polygraphs, but it is quite effective as an intimidation tool and as a deterrent against leaks.” He says that many officials are “still nervous” about doing routine polygraph tests, even if they have “nothing to hide.” “They know it's something that you can fail and that can then disrupt your career or suspend your clearance if you can't pass it, even if it's just because you were nervous that day,” Mann continues. He says that this polygraph hunt is a way for Hegseth to test the loyalty of his employees amid pressure over the continuation of efforts in Iran. Just this week, Republican Senator Thom Tillis of North Carolina urged Trump to replace Hegseth as Defense Secretary. “Independent of the Iran war, [Hegseth] has been trying quite hard, in my view, to streamline that bureaucracy to more personal loyalty—often at the expense of readiness or combat effectiveness,” Mann says. “This is one of the products of somebody who is trying to have a very, very tight grip on the bureaucracy he leads.”

Lee Chang Dong's Possible Love Is a Delicate Drama of Love and Longing in Anxious Times
2026-09-06

Lee Chang Dong's Possible Love Is a Delicate Drama of Love and Longing in Anxious Times

TIME | 2026-09-06

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings (L to R) Sul Kyung-gu as Ho-seok, Jeon Do-yeon as Mi-ok, Cho Yeo-jeong as Ye-ji, Zo In-sung as Sang-woo in Possible Love Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Lee Chang-dong’s delicate yet sturdy drama Possible Love—premiering here at the Venice Film Festival, hitting theaters Oct. 23 and streaming on Netflix Nov. 6—is a movie about figuring out how to function in the world. If that description seems confoundingly vague, Lee’s movie is not. It shows us a world where people feel lost after losing their livelihood, where individuals strive to connect with others despite differences of class and temperament, where tenderness ultimately has more power than arrogance. It’s a movie with a gentle, generous spirit. Lee opens the picture with a funeral, or, rather, a small family piling into their car to get to the funeral venue. Wife and mother Mi-ok (Jeon Do-yeon) wonders if she’s wearing the right dress, and the shoes she has chosen, a pair she hasn’t worn in a long time, are already pinching her feet. Her husband, Ho-seok (Sul Kyung-gu), says little—so little that it’s clear he’s filled with dread over whatever awaits him at this upcoming, obviously unhappy social event. Their extremely quiet young son (Jung Seop) sits in the back seat, amusing himself by dangling an empty chip bag out the window like a windsock. “It’s flying,” he says, just before letting it go—he’s not the only one feeling the oppressiveness of that car ride. It’s never expressly spelled out who this funeral is for, but we can intuit that it’s one of Ho-seok’s former work colleagues. It turns out that Ho-seok is one of many workers who not so long ago lost his job at a large corporation, SP, and he hasn’t yet recovered, either financially or emotionally. Mi-ok is doing the best she can—she holds down a menial factory job—but her husband’s depression and excessive drinking is getting to her. By chance, in the funeral home parking lot, she meets a handsome stranger, Sang-woo (Zo In-sung), who moves his very expensive car when Mi-ok believes her wedding ring, having slipped off her finger, has rolled beneath it. It turns out that Sang-woo’s wife, Ye-ji (Cho Yeo-Jong), is making a documentary about, as she puts it, “the personal lives of laborers.” Ye-ji is thrilled to have found Mi-ok and Ho-seok as subjects—she’s particularly interested in the melancholic Ho-seok, who, she believes, could be the dramatic key her project. But she and Mi-ok also genuinely like each other and strike up a friendship. Possible Love traces the tangled dynamics between these four characters, while also flirting with uncomfortable questions about the moral quandaries that can arise in any filmmaker-subject relationship. Zo In-sung as Sang-woo, Jeon Do-yeon as Mi-ok Courtesy of Netflix Mostly, though, Possible Love traces the ways people come to care for one another, either platonically or romantically. Ye-ji and Mi-ok quickly come to think of themselves as sisters. Though Ye-ji has married well, she doesn’t come from a privileged family—her father ran a dry-cleaning establishment—and she has some sense of Mi-ok’s money worries and feelings of insecurity. At an early meeting, where Ye-ji outlines the aim of her proposed documentary to Mi-ok, she leaves the table momentarily, and Mi-ok snaps a picture of the elegant backpack she’s left behind. Then she uses a Google-image search to find out the brand. It’s made by Loro Piana, and quite obviously beyond Mi-ok’s means. That's just one example of how Lee explores simple human wants, desires, jealousies. We often can't help wanting things we can't afford, especially when they've already been stamped by the approval of someone we admire. Lee has dedicated the film to the memory of Krzysztof Kieślowski’s Dekalog 10; he has said he was particularly struck by the commandment “Thou shalt not covet.” As humans, we often like nice things, but what we really need is a way to keep a roof over our head and food on the table. Possible Love explores the desperation that can strike when we can’t meet those basic needs. Sang-woo, cocksure and a little clueless, spends much of the movie on the phone making business deals. Meanwhile, Ho-seok suffers quietly, as if encased in a bubble of isolation. Mi-ok loves him, but she’s frustrated with him; no wonder she’s drawn, perhaps romantically, to sexy and stalwart Sang-woo. The movie’s most potent scene occurs late in the film when, with Ye-ji’s camera rolling, Ho-seok finally lets his guard down: Ye-ji may be getting the footage she set out for, but has she earned it honestly? Sul Kyung-gu is sensational here. If the character he’s playing is key to Ye-ji’s movie-within-a-movie, he’s also Possible Love’s anguished beating heart. Lee’s 2018 noir mystery Burning was, what we used to call in the days before the pandemic, a surprise art-house hit. An art-house hit today may not mean the same thing as it did then, given how much the cinema-going audience has shrunk in less than 10 years. It’s hard to predict whether Possible Love will find a similarly enthusiastic audience. This is perhaps a more plainspoken movie than Burning; its moral questions are fairly easy to identify and parse. But at a time when more people worldwide are worrying about hanging onto gainful employment, even as a relatively small handful of people control the majority of the world’s wealth, the dramatic pull of Possible Love may be even greater than that of Burning. Sometimes it’s a relief when a movie captures undercurrents of anxiety that, in our daily lives, we find difficult to put into words. With Possible Love, it’s as if Lee has taken our unspoken feelings and handed them to his actors. With words and gestures, with tentative smiles and flashes of anger, they show us fears and worries we might be feeling ourselves, possibly galvanizing us to face them.
