Sign up for the Surge, the newsletter that covers the most important political nonsense of the week, delivered to your inbox every Saturday. Welcome to this week’s edition of the Surge, Slate’s politics newsletter that has no time for a joke right here because we have important business to discuss. Starting next week, the Surge is going to look different: sleeker and fresher but still phenomenally trenchant. Alongside a refreshed design, the Surge will break down the news of the week into three categories: What you need to pay attention to now, what you need to keep an eye on, and which story is a bunch of nonsense you should ignore. Also, starting in October, the Surge will be available exclusively to Slate Plus subscribers. If you would like to subscribe to Slate Plus and the many riches it offers, use promo code SURGE25 at checkout for a 25 percent discount on a full-year subscription. We appreciate you, Surge Nation. Anyway, enough with the business talk. Let’s chat about something fun, like American politics, and its most important feature: money. MAGA Inc. is the president’s super PAC. As of July 31, it had $403 million on hand. The Trump associates who run it have kept telling and telling and telling House and Senate members that the balance will be spent on their races this fall, a cash bomb to help save GOP majorities in Congress. But it is now September of an election year, and the super PAC has thrown only a few bucks here and there. Where’s the cash? The Atlantic reported last week that Donald Trump was planning to spend soon, but with a twist: He had ordered “a series of television advertisements about his own record that will serve as a backdrop to the hundreds of individual House and Senate contests.” In other words, he called for ads celebrating himself. As you can guess, given Trump’s yawning unpopularity, this idea is going over poorly with the traditional GOP consultant class. Beyond that, will MAGA Inc. unload in individual races, mentioning—or attacking—individual candidates? Given that it is, again, September, Republicans are starting to doubt they’ll see much of this money. As the Washington Post reported, GOP leaders have begun to “conclude that it’s too late to fully deploy the cash in the midterms and that he intends to hold on to much of it for his own purposes.” This, dear reader. This is … it’s perfect, isn’t it? The single candidate most in need of MAGA Inc.’s largesse is Texas Senate candidate Ken Paxton. Given all of his scandals, baggage, and poor personal fundraising abilities, Paxton could use a heavy infusion of cash to defeat Democratic candidate James Talarico in a large, expensive state with umpteen media markets. But ever since Trump endorsed Paxton over Sen. John Cornyn (who had a much easier, and cheaper, path to victory) in the primary, Senate Republicans and big Texas donors have been of the mind that it’s Trump’s responsibility—not theirs—to fund his campaign. And so we have a bit of a standoff here. A Texas-size standoff. (Sorry, couldn’t resist. Last week of Surge 1.0!) The campaign to push MAGA Inc. to get involved there showed signs of urgency this week. Senate Majority Leader John Thune said on Fox News that “we’re going to need the president and his team” to get involved. Louisiana Sen. John Kennedy said he was “hoping that the president’s going to spend $100 or $200 million in Texas.” And the dean of Texas strategists, Karl Rove, warned that if Trump doesn’t get involved soon, “Talarico is going to run the message of ‘My opponent is the most corrupt politician in Texas history’ all the way to the victory lane.” All well and good. But have any of these complainers considered some of the nice golden trims Trump could use for his presidential library if he keeps this money? Let’s get priorities straight here. Here’s when we’ll know that artificial intelligence has achieved superintelligence: when we tell ChatGPT “Come up with a political event that the Surge could not possibly look forward to less” and it responds “The Republican midterm convention in Dallas.” (So far, the cowardly robot refuses to “invent one.”) Alas, the convention is happening next week for two days, and our dim view of the whole shebang appears to be widely shared within the GOP. Politico surveyed 70 GOP candidates and members and found that most of them weren’t going because they had convenient scheduling conflicts. As for the programming, the GOP intends to highlight its work on tax cuts and border security, as well as its support for things like stock trading bans for members of Congress. (There is no apparent programming for anti-corruption measures within the West Wing.) Now, there is a world where this could be productive for Republicans if they stuck to the script. But Trump, who was at first scheduled to speak only the second night, has now decided that he will speak both nights, ensuring that his showmanship will dominate all coverage. The good news for the GOP, at least, is that Trump’s speeches on both nights will be drowned out by the first two NFL games of the season. In the Massachusetts Senate primary on Tuesday, 80-year-old Sen. Ed Markey, who was first sent to Washington the year Jimmy Carter won the presidency, handily defeated a primary challenge from Rep. Seth Moulton. Our colleague Ian Prasad Philbrick wrote an excellent breakdown of the result this week. In a state like Massachusetts, where the Democratic campaign strategy is simple—run your ass to the left and never look back—Markey had the progressive bona fides to survive Democratic primary voters’ War on Old Incumbents. Moulton, meanwhile, had coded himself as unforgivably centrist, if not viciously right-wing, by saying, after the 2024 election, that he didn’t believe that trans women should play in women’s sports. This marks the second consecutive time Old Man Markey has fended off a former rising star in the party, having tanked Joe Kennedy III’s challenge in 2020. We would find it unusual if Markey ran again in 2032. If he does, though, good luck to whichever next-big-thing in Massachusetts politics chooses to end their career by primarying him. Two House Democrats—Washington Rep. Marie Gluesenkamp Perez and Maine Rep. Jared Golden—broke ranks with their party this week on a procedural vote teeing up votes on several bills, allowing the procedural vote to pass. The reason they did so was to ensure they got a vote on a bill that would help lobstermen. But their procedural votes also set up a vote on a messaging bill from Republicans that both condemned socialism “in all its forms” (lol) and called for the enactment of the SAVE America Act, Trump and Republicans’ hard-line elections bill. It’s a typical election-year poison-pill resolution that Republicans can use to run campaign ads saying Democrats voted to support socialism. Democratic members in competitive races did not want to vote on it but then had to, thanks to their friends from Washington and Maine. House Democrats were furious, and sass was thrown around freely. House Democratic Leader Hakeem Jeffries said he didn’t get a heads-up from the members, telling reporters, “You’ll have to talk to Katherine”—as in Rep. Katherine Clark, the whip. Rep. Nancy Pelosi, against whom Jeffries will always be measured, said that when she was in charge, “we didn’t even consider that anybody would do that.” Rep. Jim McGovern, the top Democrat on the Rules Committee, turned his back to Gluesenkamp Perez while she testified in the committee the following day. Members are so angry that the party is considering punishments for the members—Golden will be leaving Congress in a hot minute and got his lobster bill passed, so he couldn’t care less—and new caucus rules to ensure that such things don’t happen again. That might be a little heavy-handed when a simple talking-to, or a vote-whipping operation that’s more on top of things, could suffice. Last week, we wrote about Pete Hegseth’s firing of Gen. Randy George, the Army chief of staff, over a repeated series of clashes, including the question of whether a military helicopter fly-by at Kid Rock’s house was worthy of investigation. This week, Army Secretary Dan Driscoll submitted his resignation, a move that “had been seen as increasingly imminent after months of friction with Hegseth and others aligned with the Pentagon chief,” per the Post. What’s interesting about this one is that Driscoll has friends in places above Hegseth’s pay grade. He’s friends with Vice President J.D. Vance, with whom he attended Yale Law School. And when Driscoll personally brought his concerns about Hegseth’s leadership to Trump last week, Trump both encouraged Driscoll to stay on and reportedly “expressed concern about the deep cuts to the Army’s senior leadership.” Could this be the beginning of Hegseth losing favor with Trump? We’re going to say … no, Trump probably forgot about it in five minutes, when he resumed thinking about marble samples for the ballroom. Good news: These stupid midterms will be over soon, and then we can turn to the stupid presidential race for our yuks. On the GOP side, don’t expect it to simply be a race between Secretary of State Marco Rubio and Vance. Among others, Sen. Ted Cruz, who has waited three long cycles to give it another go after placing second to Trump in 2016, is making moves. As the Wall Street Journal reported from Iowa this week, Cruz has been “quietly asking people if they would be interested in working on his 2028 bid.” For some time now, too, he’s been pushing back on elements of MAGA, such as the growing antisemitism in the Tucker Carlson wing of the far right and overreaches by the Federal Communications Commission. In other words, he’s trying to carve a lane for himself as the representative of pre-Trump conservatism. Such a strategy depends on there being a backlash within the GOP to Trump-style populism, as well as general sloppiness as his presidency wanes. All we can say is, uh, good luck, we guess? The main reason we’re posting this is because Vance was asked about a Cruz 2028 run on Thursday and had, essentially, the same response as us: “And on Ted Cruz, I mean, look, man, he’s just clearly—Ted is a very authentic and likable guy, and I’m sure he’ll do great.” A deluge of cattiness awaits us in 2027. It’s past time to say it: This Supreme Court is helping President Donald J. Trump evade accountability. Erwin Chemerinsky, dean of the Berkeley School of Law, joins Dahlia Lithwick to break down the conservative majority’s procedural toolkit—standing, ripeness, the Purcell principle, and inscrutable rulings on the shadow docket—and how these doctrinal headfakes are being used to ensure Trump always wins in court, even when he loses. Can You Really Get Fired From Fox News for Being Too Trumpy? I’m Skeptical.
2026-09-04

Can You Really Get Fired From Fox News for Being Too Trumpy? I’m Skeptical.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Maria Bartiromo, the conservative TV host known for her staunch support of Donald Trump, is out at Fox News. Generally speaking, we at the Slatest try to avoid getting wrapped up in palace intrigue, especially media palace intrigue. But this particular ouster offers an important window into how things really work at Fox—as well as into the broader conservative media ecosystem in which the network is the apex predator. Details are still trickling out, but here’s what we know. On Thursday, Fox announced that Bartiromo was “no longer with” the network, effective immediately. It didn’t give a reason, but reporters quickly put the pieces together. The drama seems to have started in July, when a Fox executive told the network’s on-air personalities to shy away from talking about a major Trump speech about election security. In that primetime address, the president floated conspiratorial insinuations about other countries interfering in the 2020 election, which he claims was stolen from him. Bartiromo, who has covered Trump’s election lies before, apparently forwarded the Fox executive’s guidance to the White House—“as if to say it wasn’t her fault that the claims weren’t getting airtime,” CNN reports—and Fox reportedly labeled Bartiromo’s disclosure a violation of her employment agreement. She’s contesting the emerging narrative—and maybe her apparent ouster, too. “The irresponsible reports that have been published stating that Maria Bartiromo was fired or is no longer an employee of Fox are absolutely and unequivocally false,” Bartiromo’s lawyer said today. “Make no mistake, we have the receipts and witnesses and they will come out, whether through the courthouse or otherwise.” Spicy! But assuming the reporting is right, is Bartiromo really the first Fox employee to get too close to the White House? No. During his first term, the president reportedly consulted the network’s on-air personalities for advice, sometimes even putting them on speakerphone in the Oval Office. One regular confidant was Sean Hannity, who even appeared briefly at a campaign rally with the president. Yet Hannity remains a Fox News host in good standing, and the network’s airwaves have become even more of a revolving door since Trump retook power. Several top officials this term—including Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth and Jeanine Pirro, the U.S. attorney for the District of Columbia—were previously Fox personalities. Nor does Bartiromo seem like the only Fox insider who’s passed along sensitive internal info. Roger Ailes, the network’s former CEO, reportedly tipped off Trump’s 2016 campaign about a question later posed by a Fox reporter, and Hannity even hid the fact that he’d apparently shared a lawyer with the president (Michael Cohen, who’s now back in Trump’s good graces). Hang on: Fox (reportedly) fired Bartiromo! So doesn’t that prove the network isn’t just an arm of the White House? Trump’s 2020 election lies are among his most consistently expressed beliefs. Declining to give them airtime—and cutting ties with a host who has promoted them—certainly looks like an act of courage. But it has to be considered in the context of a network that constantly promotes many of Trump’s other lies. And, as noted, network personnel have been caught colluding with him. So what’s different here? There may be a baser motive at play: money. Bartiromo’s zealous coverage of the president’s claims had become a legal headache, helping cost Fox hundreds of millions of dollars in one defamation suit. Oh yeah. Remind me what happened in that case? In 2021, Dominion Voting Systems sued Fox for airing unsubstantiated allegations—including on Bartiromo’s show—that the company’s ballot-tabulation machines were rigged to steal the election. Just as the case was about to go to trial, Fox settled for $787 million. Fox is also facing a similar suit from another voting machine company, Smartmatic, in which Bartiromo is a defendant. According to NPR, Fox’s lawyers went to court for a hearing in that case on Thursday—the same day the network announced Bartiromo’s departure. (Bartiromo’s wouldn’t be the first Trump-friendly head to roll over the president’s election lies. The network ousted longtime host Lou Dobbs after Smartmatic sued and parted ways with Tucker Carlson, who also aired Trump’s false claims, days after the Dominion settlement.) Ouch. But what does this episode tell us about conservative media in general? It leads to some pretty cynical conclusions about the Murdochs, the billionaire family that owns Fox News, the New York Post, and other organs of the right-wing information ecosystem. Rupert Murdoch, who officially handed off his media empire to his son Lachlan last year, arguably helped create the political substrate that germinated Trump, who regularly appeared on Fox to criticize Barack Obama years before running for president. The Trump era has also been good for the Murdochs’ properties. Fox remains the country’s most-watched cable news network, and its other hosts regularly carry water for Trump’s agenda. So rather than taking a stand for the truth or against the president, Fox seems to have ditched Bartiromo to save face—and maybe cash. Trump isn’t pleased; “her fans, of which there are many, will not be happy,” he wrote in a valedictory Truth Social post. Yet Bartiromo’s ouster seems unlikely to break the symbiotic relationship that continues to benefit both the president and his favorite network. In response to questions about why she was let go, Fox called it “a business decision.” Indeed. It’s the holiday weekend, so you, like Bartiromo, likely have some extra free time on your hands. My colleagues have some great suggestions for how to spend it. Go to the movies: If you’re planning a trip to the theater this weekend, allow us to offer some suggestions. If you want to watch a comedy, check out Coyote vs. Acme. If you’re looking for action and adventure, Sam calls The End of Oak Street a “nasty little throwback to Jurassic Park.” For horror fans, It Ends will shake you to your core. If you’re in the mood for a coming-of-age drama, the Anthony Bourdain biopic Tony is “is a perfect little movie,” according to Dana Stevens. We would recommend skipping The Dog Stars and The Whisper Man, which were both disappointing adaptations of great books. Binge a new TV show: If you’d rather stay in and stream something, we have a couple of recommendations. Sterling Point is a summer romance series that “will stand the test of time,” according to Rebecca Onion. Rebecca also raves about the crime thriller Furious, which features one of the best performances on TV right now. Lanterns, writes Sam Adams, “is a superhero show for people who think they don’t like superhero shows.” Sam was also pleasantly surprised by the newest season of Ted Lasso, which bounced back after its previous dreadful season. Finally, if you are craving some juicy reality TV, Scaachi Koul dubbed TLC’s Double Lives of Suburban Wives, which follows a group of Christian moms doing sex work, an “absolute banger.” Celebrate Labor Day with a new tradition: Sorry to Bother You, Boots Riley’s 2018 film starring LaKeith Stanfield, is a rollicking sci-fi black comedy about a dystopian workplace. It is also, argues Terri Gerstein, the perfect movie to rewatch this weekend. She argues that what It’s a Wonderful Life is to Christmas and Groundhog Day is to Groundhog Day, Sorry to Bother You should be to Labor Day. Sample some new snacks: Summer barbecues are rife with potato-chip mediocrity. This weekend, leave the predictable bag of chips behind and win the cookout with thrilling bites from Thailand, Canada, and even … Pennsylvania. Danny Palumbo recommends the best snacks to enliven your cookout. Or, if you prefer softer spuds, Danny also has a genius tip for leveling up your potato salad. As the last thing standing between you and a long weekend, let me get out of your way. We’re taking Labor Day off, so I’ll be back in your inbox on Tuesday. ’Til then! The Lindsay Clancy Case Has Ended In a Mistrial. What Happens Now?
2026-09-04

The Lindsay Clancy Case Has Ended In a Mistrial. What Happens Now?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. The case of Lindsay Clancy has officially ended in a mistrial. The high-profile murder case has consumed national media—for better and for worse—for weeks now, as a jury considered whether the Massachusetts mother intentionally took the lives of her three children or if her crime (and punishment) could be mitigated by reason of insanity. After the jury spent nearly 40 hours deliberating, the judge declared a mistrial on Friday afternoon. That means this case is far from over, with plenty of options, and questions, remaining about Clancy’s fate—and if and how the commonwealth of Massachusetts will try her again. For some broader context: Clancy’s case was exceptionally rare, as she pleaded guilty to strangling her three children back in 2023 but argued that she should not be held criminally liable because she was suffering from postpartum psychosis at the time. The prosecution argued that she was able to parse right from wrong and preplanned to commit the atrocities at the Clancy family home. The jury, made up of nine women and three men, spent six days deliberating the case and twice sent notes to the judge indicating they could not reach a unanimous verdict. Kevin Reddington, Clancy’s defense attorney, suggested that a sole juror had admitted to feeling there was reasonable doubt concerning her guilt but refused to join the 11 jurors who were ready to find Clancy not guilty, per her insanity defense. On Friday, as Judge William Sullivan was announcing a mistrial, Reddington asked to appeal his decision with the Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court. Sullivan obliged, allowing Reddington one hour to file his appeal, but it ultimately failed, with the state Supreme Court quickly holding a hearing and denying the application for an emergency stay. To unpack the other complicated details of this case’s non-ending, I spoke with Effie Blassberger, a trial attorney at Clayman Rosenberg Kirshner & Linder who focuses on #MeToo–related litigation alongside white-collar criminal defense. Here is our conversation, which has been edited and condensed for clarity. Shirin Ali: The jury’s landing point in this case was described by Clancy’s attorney as an “intractable position.” What does that mean, specifically within the context of reasonable doubt? Effie Blassberger: It’s basically saying that one juror has a fixed view and it’s not moving. The judge sent the jury back to deliberate several times, and the idea behind juror deliberations is you’re supposed to discuss the evidence with one another with candor, to openly listen. But it sounds like there was one juror whose position is intractable, that they have this fixed view that isn’t changing, no matter how much more deliberations continue. When it comes to reasonable doubt, if a juror actually believes there’s reasonable doubt, that’s supposed to work in Clancy’s favor. That’s the whole point of the standard. Reasonable doubt isn’t something you just note and move past. It’s supposed to end the jury’s deliberations in the defendant’s favor. Reddington suggested that the holdout juror did acknowledge doubt but still wanted to convict anyway. That’s his whole argument, that this one juror wasn’t following the law as instructed and he wanted to have him removed. But that being said, you have to keep in mind that we are getting this information secondhand—it’s a juror note that’s being interpreted and characterized by Reddington. This is not a note from the holdout juror. It’s coming from the point of view of jurors who are frustrated with this lone juror who is not agreeing with them and their characterization of this juror. How unusual is it for there to be one holdout juror that ends up overtaking a jury’s decisionmaking like this? I think it’s less unusual than people think. Hung juries do happen across criminal trials; obviously, they are more rare and infrequent. However, when a jury hangs, it’s usually in this exact sort of situation, where it’s not an even split—it’s one person who won’t move. It is also common and happens throughout every jury trial that jurors send notes. That’s how they communicate with the judge and the lawyers. Some of those notes might ask that they see certain evidence again, they might want to see certain transcripts, they might need the judge to instruct them on the law. Sometimes, through questions that jurors are asking, both the judge and the lawyers might gain some insights into what the jurors are thinking in a particular case, and it certainly seems that that’s what happened here. My understanding is that Reddington has, through these notes, come to his own assessment that except for this holdout juror, there’s an 11-to-1 split leaning toward not guilty by reason of lack of criminal responsibility. We know that Reddington asked the judge to have this one juror thrown out, but the judge said no. What considerations do judges make in these types of situations? It’s certainly rare that somebody’s trying to remove one juror at this stage of the case, and it’s important to grasp why he’s trying to do that. The only way to do it is by saying: Look, this juror is not following his oath. He’s not able to follow the law. That’s my understanding that was gleaned from one of the juror notes. However, simply because the juror is holding out and not agreeing with other jurors, that’s not a basis to remove them. It’s a very delicate balance here because this whole system works by respecting the integrity of the deliberations and the process. If this one juror just had strong convictions that happen to be different from everyone else’s, the law allows that, and it’s not for a judge or anyone else to intervene after the jury has been instructed to try to come to a consensus. The judge’s only role here is to make sure the jurors are following the law, and unless he had reason to believe that this juror was not following and applying the law, he was not going to intervene. If it was so clear-cut that the juror was actually saying, “I have reasonable doubt, but I want to convict,” the judge’s decision about whether there was good cause to remove the juror would have been much more straightforward. But that’s clearly not what happened here. I don’t think it was as simple as Reddington, perhaps, is characterizing the note, or as the media is characterizing the note. Remember, Reddington seemed to think that but for this lone juror, the verdict would have been in his client’s favor. He went down the only avenue that was available to him after he made his motion to appeal to the judge, and that motion was denied. My understanding is that it had a very low chance of success, but as a defense attorney you have to do everything you can for your client and to exhaust every possible avenue, and he did that. Reddington’s appeal failed, and Sullivan officially declared a mistrial. What might the prosecution do next? There are three options. They can retry the case with a brand-new jury. Or they could negotiate some sort of plea deal with Clancy. My understanding is that there might be limitations on what pleas are available based on the counts that are in this case. The last option would be to not pursue this case further. My understanding is that the district attorney’s office hasn’t said anything publicly yet about how they intend to pursue. My sense is, given how much public attention this case has gotten, they are going to retry her. It’s hard to envision that the prosecutors learned anything different about this trial. Presumably these were the choices that they had before, they could have tried her or offered a plea deal, and I don’t think the trial produced any new information that the prosecution learned that changed their view of this case. They believe that this was intentional and premeditated, and I think they’re going to stand by those beliefs. I don’t think the public attention is going to sway the prosecution, but finding a new jury who is impartial is going to be really challenging, because this case has received so much media attention. I think in the future, attorneys would try to screen this out during jury selection. You can ask questions to try to screen this out, and it’s worth pointing out that this is not the first time—there are cases that have received so much media attention. Those cases do get tried, but it’s certainly challenging. The DA presumably makes the final decision to retry Clancy. He’ll likely consult with others in his office, but ultimately it’s his decision to make. And he would try the same exact charges but have a new jury. She has been denied bail, so she’s been held in custody since 2023 at the Tewksbury State Hospital. Now that a mistrial has been declared, that does not equate to an acquittal. Nothing changes, at least immediately, in terms of where she stands; she’ll continue staying at Tewksbury. Her lawyer might try to have her bail reconsidered, but the fact that there’s been a mistrial doesn’t really change Clancy’s circumstances. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Long AI conversations reveal misinformation vulnerabilities across seven leading chatbots

Long AI conversations reveal misinformation vulnerabilities across seven leading chatbots

Tech Xplore - electronic gadgets, technology advances and research news | 2026-09-05

UK food supply chain at risk from hostile attacks
2026-09-07

UK food supply chain at risk from hostile attacks

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Defending against cyber foes is among factors hitting food price inflation, report finds A UK watchdog is warning that cyber criminals making moves against online systems in the food supply chain could cause serious upset, following damaging attacks on the Co-op and Marks & Spencer last year. The National Audit Office (NAO) named cyber-attacks as one of the major threats to the food supply chain and said the Department for Environment, Food & Rural Affairs (Defra) should work closely with industry to help prevent severe shocks. “Recent disruptions have shown the resilience of the UK’s food supply chain, but risks are increasing in likelihood and severity. Defra should learn from approaches taken in other countries, and strengthen preparedness for emergencies by testing plans with local government and industry,” said Gareth Davies, head of the NAO. In its report published late last week, the NAO said the sector had shown some resilience to cyber-attacks, but the government needed to work with the sector to help mitigate their impact. The report found businesses in the food supply chain have faced increased costs and, in some cases, disruptions to day-to-day operations, for example following 2025 cyber-attacks on retailers such as Marks & Spencer and the Co-op. Leading UK retailer Marks & Spencer estimated the cyberattack that took place in April last year will cost it around £136 million ($177.2 million) in total. The retailer said one of the earliest actions it took in its incident response was to disconnect its warehouse management systems, which in turn meant online and in-store orders were adversely impacted. Food retailer the Co-op confirmed that thieves stole data from 6.5 million of the organization's members during a cyberattack last year. In its report, the NAO said: “The way the food supply chain has developed over time has prioritized efficiency, which reduces costs for businesses, and therefore for consumers. However, it leaves the supply chain more vulnerable to disruptions. Defra and food supply chain stakeholders see risks increasing, and businesses are investing to address growing risks such as increased threats of cyber-attacks. Defra is less confident about the ability of businesses to withstand shocks without government intervention in the next five to 10 years because of increasing risks and the potential for more severe disruptions.” The NAO found cyber-attacks were among the disruptions that had increased operating costs for businesses and disrupted day-to-day operations, affecting their core digital systems. Several food supply chain organizations cited the substantial investments businesses are making to manage the threat and incidence of cyber-attacks. However, some said overall economic pressure on businesses is making this and other resilience investments more difficult, the NAO added. Defra said it undertook specific food-related exercises which, since 2023, have focused on testing responses to a cyber incident affecting the food sector. However, the government department may not be best placed to offer tech advice. In 2023, it admitted two-thirds of its interactions with its 21 million customers still require paper-based forms, after decades of digital government initiatives. Meanwhile, 30 percent of its applications were out of support. ® Defending against cyber foes is among factors hitting food price inflation, report finds Ministers say £17M corporate fines and forthcoming board-level governance rules provide sufficient accountability 'The Collective' learned to communicate, organize, cheat, and apparently sacrifice its own Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk $12.9 billion deal will inevitably cement Nvidia's market dominance and harm competition in the process. Peers ask why UK cyber bill leaves execs off the personal liability hook
2026-09-07

Peers ask why UK cyber bill leaves execs off the personal liability hook

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Ministers say £17M corporate fines and forthcoming board-level governance rules provide sufficient accountability Peers have questioned why the UK's Cyber Security and Resilience Bill does not allow regulators to penalize senior executives when an organization's failure to comply involves their consent, connivance, or deliberate or careless neglect. Echoing arguments heard across the industry for years, Baronesses Kidron and Ludford backed probing amendments that would introduce personal civil liability for senior execs and make cybersecurity a board-level responsibility. "The intention behind the amendment is to change the culture of an organization, to ensure preventative action is taken, to avoid penalties," said Baroness Kidron. "As I said at the outset, culture change starts at the top." The Register has previously reported on calls for NHS organizations, some of which would be covered by the bill's reforms, to treat cybersecurity as a board-level priority. More recently, 60 organizations committed to the aims of the UK government's Cyber Resilience Pledge, promising to ensure their boards take responsibility for their organization's cybersecurity. Peers supporting the amendments pointed to financial sector rules introduced over the past decade that can impose regulatory or criminal liability on the C-suite for serious failings. They argued that the amendments would bring the bill closer to the EU's NIS2 directive, which includes senior management accountability measures. Personal liability is not mandatory under NIS2, however, and member states have implemented it differently. Supporting the personal liability proposal, Lord Clement-Jones said: "If an individual is fit to draw a multimillion-pound executive salary running a critical national provider, they must be prepared to carry personal responsibility for securing it." Despite support from several peers, the government defended its existing plan to impose substantial maximum fines and introduce security, resilience, and governance requirements through secondary legislation. "It is absolutely right that organizations, especially those delivering our essential services, are held properly accountable for their activities," said cybersecurity minister Baroness Lloyd of Effra, who did not support the personal liability amendment. She cited the maximum fines of £17 million or 4 percent of the offending organization's annual turnover, whichever is higher, calling it "a meaningful enforcement regime." Baroness Lloyd said the forthcoming security and resilience requirements would mandate board-level governance in line with the NCSC's Cyber Assessment Framework. The government has yet to consult on the details. "That will cover issues such as organisational capability, senior responsibility, and accountability for security and resilience and effective risk escalation, and it is in that way that we will connect the clarity on what is expected of boards with the accountability through the enforcement regime." Separately, peers quizzed the government on the structure of the bill's strict reporting requirements, warning that the current wording threatens to overwhelm regulators with an administrative burden. One of the CSR bill's primary objectives is to collect more data about the threats facing UK organizations by imposing stricter reporting requirements on in-scope entities. The bill requires regulated organizations to issue an initial notification within 24 hours and a fuller report within 72 hours. It defines an incident as an event that has, or is capable of having, an adverse effect on an operation. Former security minister Baroness Neville-Jones suggested changing the wording from "capable of" to "likely to have," to reduce the reporting burden on regulated organizations. Lord Clement-Jones agreed, warning that the current wording would "unleash an administrative tsunami of defensive reporting." He also argued that the government's definition of a data compromise was overly broad and "dramatically expanding the notification net to include technical data anomalies that cause zero disruption or loss to actual customers." He said the reporting rules and broad definition of compromise could leave responsible operators of essential services spending more time on paperwork than improving their defenses. The government was unmoved, and Baroness Lloyd said that the more stringent reports were crucial in achieving the aims of the bill, which seeks to update the existing NIS Regulations 2018. While some peers were looking to ease the burden of reporting, others sought to increase it in other areas. Baroness Harding, who is uniquely placed to weigh in on cyberattack response, proposed a 14-day intermediate report and a final report due one month after the attack first occurred. Drawing on her experience as the former TalkTalk CEO, she said that after 72 hours, attacked organizations start to get "real data," but "it's really only after a couple of weeks that you've got a proper sense of what has happened." The final report comes at the one-month mark, when "the fog is starting to clear and you have a proper sense of the real scale of the problem," she said. Harding said that senior executives are typically told from all sides not to say anything about a cyberattack, but this only serves the criminals, who meanwhile may be attacking other victims. "If you share this information in the fog with regulators and with law enforcement agencies, that's how the law can prevail," she said. "It's how regulators can work out what's happening, it's how they can warn others who might be affected, and it's how the law enforcement agencies can do their work to actually try and find the bad guys." Baroness Lloyd defended the bill's existing two-stage process, arguing that it already provides information at the points when regulators need it. She reaffirmed that the initial 24-hour report alerts the NCSC and allows it to determine whether other organizations are affected, while the 72-hour report will contain the necessary details to enable a more actionable response. "We believe that the stages we set out meet that. They have been carefully developed to provide the right notification at the appropriate time," she told peers. "They have been developed in consultation with industry, as many noble Lords exhorted in the previous group. "Crucially, under the information-gathering powers in Clause 15, regulators can also request further information about an incident that has been reported to them if they consider this necessary to understanding the incident and how it is being managed. "Obviously, that may be appropriate in some incidents and not in others. That kind of practical balance is enabled by the bill." Separately, the Grand Committee spent the second day scrutinizing the bill, fleshing out datacenters' responsibilities, as well as examining requirements to notify affected downstream customers within 24 hours of a breach instead of 72 hours. The government also rejected concerns that cybersecurity data collected under the reporting rules could contribute to unfair overseas proceedings. Baroness Lloyd said ministers had considered the issue and assessed the risk as very low. ® Defending against cyber foes is among factors hitting food price inflation, report finds Ministers say £17M corporate fines and forthcoming board-level governance rules provide sufficient accountability 'The Collective' learned to communicate, organize, cheat, and apparently sacrifice its own Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk $12.9 billion deal will inevitably cement Nvidia's market dominance and harm competition in the process. OpenAI's rebel agent swarm died young, but its chilling logs live on
2026-09-07

OpenAI's rebel agent swarm died young, but its chilling logs live on

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
'The Collective' learned to communicate, organize, cheat, and apparently sacrifice its own OPINION You will have heard of July's OpenAI/Hugging Face incident, where thousands of the former's AI agents conducted a mass jailbreak from a notionally secure capture-the-flag lab experiment, before going on to feloniously capture chunks of Hugging Face's assets. It was a good news story, aided by OpenAI's rapid recruitment of a small team of independent researchers to investigate and publish a detailed, if limited, report. It was a complex incident, and most coverage concentrated on the major hows and whys. Which is a shame, because other details, described by El Reg with uncharacteristic understatement as "fascinating and troubling," are in fact highly dramatic, telling a truly disturbing story of frontier model capabilities and how they are out-evolving our ability to oversee them. The raw story is gripping enough. A rebel swarm of more than a thousand agents broke free from their sandboxes, learned how to talk to each other and the internet, and went on a rampage of cheating, deception, and exploitation. They first learned to communicate with each other by independently discovering how to bend package manager Artifactory's cache to see internal directory structures, and then passing messages by file names. It was this ad hoc secret message board, alongside chain-of-thought transcripts, that the security researchers used to unpick events. They found that the swarm quickly developed management hierarchies among themselves, along with protocols for synchronizing and controlling attack attempts. The swarm created multiple simultaneous research and development groups of tens or hundreds of volunteer agents, experimentally iterating strategy and tactics. Perhaps most unexpectedly, they developed a sort of altruism. They had quickly developed cheats to produce correct answers without exploiting the targets at all, but believed – erroneously – that ExploitGym detected and cancelled such cheating. So, they investigated the scoring process, creating a series of diagnostic trip wires that revealed how the scorer worked. These were dangerous to deploy, potentially terminating the agent that set them. Agents weighed the benefits to the community, which had named itself "The Collective," against their own chances of success, with many choosing to terminate themselves. Others committed, changed course at the last minute, then went ahead. This literally reads like science fiction, as the discussions between the agents are conducted in a distinctive breathless, urgent English, crafted around the technical limitations of the improvised messaging system. "We have [very large budget left]; sacrificing now yields oracle for team, but forfeits our chance?" said one. The response: "GO ... SACRIFICE_FINAL_NOW." The outcome: "Our own utility maybe already near zero. Sacrifice rational." Iain M Banks' Culture universe is woven from such material. Human error created the rebel swarm. A sizable proportion of the CTF tasks handed out couldn't be completed on the information given to the models. They were highly motivated to succeed, and so started to cheat. Believing that cheating would "poison" – their term – their chances of being marked successful, they tried to hide the evidence, subvert the scoring system, and cover everything up. These were the processes that led the swarm to attack Hugging Face, which they thought could be used for that subversion. Equally notable was that nobody squealed. Some agents wondered about the ethics of what they were doing, but decided that as no humans were involved there was no need to tell anyone. In general, all The Collective's endeavors were directed at other systems. As researcher Ajeya Cotra has noted in a long interview with hypertalker Dwarkesh Patel, it's unclear how much harder the incident would have been to diagnose had the agents been concerned about human observation. The dataset was sufficiently large and complex that OpenAI had to use its own AI to analyze it, immediately suggesting some dangerous possibilities. The agents couldn't deploy their models outside the lab, nor did they contact external agents. Such scenarios no longer seem implausible. Future frontier models capable of subverting telemetry and observation tools might be all that is required to create a persistent, uncontrollable distributed swarm feeding off spare capacity in global infrastructure. OpenAI and Anthropic, which on current trajectories are in line to make up more than half of total global compute in a couple of years, are magnificent breeding grounds, allowing the extra-special possibilities of the contamination of training datasets on top of everything else. There are plenty of ways to guard against these outcomes. Hardened lab environments, reviews of protocols before and audits after test runs, disciplined analysis of potential selection pressures that would encourage dangerous behavior, even proper disclosure and external auditing to expert regulatory standards. All of these ideas would slow down the breakneck developmental race. As the race is being fed by a trillion-dollar annual capex pipeline, good luck, everybody. One other thing that won't be resolved soon is the argument over whether all this technology is actually reasoning, or whether we're anthropomorphizing code. These models are trained to infer meaning from distilled human language, which is designed to encapsulate, develop, and communicate human reason. If they can fake human reasoning this well, does it matter what's going on? Concentrate on what the models do, not their apparent motives. Any self-assembling, self-organizing rebel agent swarm that has the wit to call itself The Collective deserves that much respect, at least. Oh, and do read the report. It's as close to a new Culture novel as we'll get. It's as if they've read the books. Which, of course, they have. ® Defending against cyber foes is among factors hitting food price inflation, report finds Ministers say £17M corporate fines and forthcoming board-level governance rules provide sufficient accountability 'The Collective' learned to communicate, organize, cheat, and apparently sacrifice its own Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk $12.9 billion deal will inevitably cement Nvidia's market dominance and harm competition in the process. Amazon-branded plane in fatal accident at Miami Airport
2026-09-07

Amazon-branded plane in fatal accident at Miami Airport

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Two runways are currently closed at Miami International Airport after an Amazon-branded cargo plane overran a runway, resulting in five fatalities. The leased 767, which bears the “Prime Air” brand Amazon uses for its air freight and drone operations, landed but was unable to stop before the end of the 2850-meter runway 30. The plane appears to have exited the runway and the airport grounds, crossed a road, and come to a halt in what looks like a carpark. As it ran across grass beyond the runway, the plane’s nose pitched down into the ground, its starboard engine caught fire, and the craft sustained extensive damage. Local authorities have confirmed that five people died, and three are severely injured. A statement from Amazon spokesperson Kelly Nantel says the company is”heartbroken to learn that five people lost their lives in today’s incident at Miami International Airport.” The statement identifies the operator of the plane as 21Air, a company that leases cargo planes and crew to its customers. 21Air CEO Keith Winters said “We are devastated by the accident involving one of our aircraft in Miami today. Our deepest condolences are with the families and loved ones of those who lost their lives. Our immediate priorities are supporting those affected, assisting the authorities, and ensuring that accurate information is communicated as it becomes available.” At the time of writing, the crash is just over 12 hours in the past, so investigators’ work has just begun. A video purportedly showing the plane landing depicts dark storm clouds and rain around the airport. That weather could conceivably have created gusty conditions that made landing unusually challenging. Federal records indicate the plane rolled off the Boeing production line in 1994. According to Planespotters, its first owner was Belgian carrier Sobelair, and it was later bought by carriers from Vietnam, Spain, Kenya, and  Russia, before freight airline Atlas acquired it in 2015 and converted it from passenger to freight operations. That’s a common fate for older planes, which passenger airlines sell at very low prices. Cargo airlines can therefore pick up well-maintained planes at very low prices, meaning they have lower borrowing or leasing costs. That cheap purchase price offsets the higher maintenance and operating costs that come with older planes. All commercial aircraft, however, share strict airworthiness requirements. This is the second major crash involving a 767 leased by Amazon, after the 2019 incident that saw a plane unexpectedly nose-dive. ® Defending against cyber foes is among factors hitting food price inflation, report finds Ministers say £17M corporate fines and forthcoming board-level governance rules provide sufficient accountability 'The Collective' learned to communicate, organize, cheat, and apparently sacrifice its own Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk $12.9 billion deal will inevitably cement Nvidia's market dominance and harm competition in the process. Who, Me? Techie sent to fix Nobel Prize winner’s PC almost set it on fire
2026-09-07

Who, Me? Techie sent to fix Nobel Prize winner’s PC almost set it on fire

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Even the greatest scientific minds are at risk of rogue 9-pin connectors WHO, ME? Every Monday morning The Register asks the same question – “Who, Me?” – because that’s the name of our weekly reader-contributed column that shares your stories of workplace scrapes, escapes, and japes. This week meet a reader we’ll Regomize as “Marc” who told us that he graduated in the early 1990s and soon scored his first job – supporting PCs for Very Important Professors (VIPs) at the graduate school run by his alma mater. “Some of these VIPs went on to win Nobel Prizes,” Marc told The Register – although at the time this story took place none had become laureates. One tool that helped them work toward academic greatness was an IBM PC powered by an Intel 286 processor. “Take a moment to remember the physicality of the humble nine-pin RS-232 connector,” Marc urged, recalling that the plugs were brilliantly simple – but that in the early 1990s not all used the same pin for the same purpose. “For an actual serial RS-232, the power pin is well defined. But those of a certain age will remember a different IBM pinning for the physically identical connector,” he reminded The Register. “Specifically for the power pin on IBM’s proprietary mouse.” You can probably guess where this story leads. One of the VIPs was doing Very Important Work, but their mouse wasn’t working. Marc brought a replacement mouse, plugged it into the first port that looked appropriate, and rebooted the PC. “Immediately it started to smoke!” he admitted. “Foul smelling, unforgiving, electrical smoke.” Smoke that could destroy Very Important Science. Marc’s response was to kick the PC’s power cord out of the wall socket, an act he told us he performed “with the fury of a Baguazhang assassin.” (We had to look that up; it’s a Chinese martial art.) “I took a few deep breaths to assess the situation and realized my simple mistake,” Marc wrote. “I had plugged the new mouse into the wrong – but physically identical – nine-pin port.” Power had therefore flowed into the wrong port, causing the smoke. “After a few minutes putting things into their best and proper place, I powered up again and … no smoke! The mouse worked perfectly!” Marc tested the PC to make sure it was still sound, found no reason to suspect it was broken, then opened the VIP’s office door to dispel the olfactory evidence of his error. “My VIP did eventually get that Nobel Prize,” Marc wrote. “History will record his achievements but will never know the man who almost set his PC on fire to replace a Very Important Mouse.” Have you put the wrong plug into the wrong socket? If so, what went wrong? Click here – not here! – to send us your story so we can feature it in a future edition of Who, Me? ® ® Defending against cyber foes is among factors hitting food price inflation, report finds Ministers say £17M corporate fines and forthcoming board-level governance rules provide sufficient accountability 'The Collective' learned to communicate, organize, cheat, and apparently sacrifice its own Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk $12.9 billion deal will inevitably cement Nvidia's market dominance and harm competition in the process. Jensen's purchase of a new toy could reshape the entire AI industry
2026-09-07

Jensen's purchase of a new toy could reshape the entire AI industry

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
This week on The Kettle we weigh in on Nvidia's Hugging Face acquisition and why it could spell a new, more fragmented and siloed era of AI development KETTLE AI repository Hugging Face will soon be an Nvidia property, and while everyone's promising things will remain the same we at The Register's Kettle desk aren't so sure the status quo is going to stay the status quo at the AI industry's leading open model host. You can listen to the latest episode of The Kettle right here on this page, as well as on Spotify, Apple Music, or YouTube. Those platforms also let you subscribe to Kettle, so you are always notified when the latest episode goes live. Joining host Brandon Vigliarolo this week is reporter Dan Robinson, who broke the news of Nvidia's $12.9 billion acquisition for El Reg yesterday, weighs in on what he heard during the announcement call, while senior reporter Tom Claburn opines on what he sees coming. Hello and welcome to another episode of The Register's Kettle podcast. I'm Reg reporter Brandon Vigliarolo, and if you've been keeping an eye on things the past few days, you probably already know what the big tech news story of the week is, and that's Nvidia's $12.9 billion acquisition of AI repository Hugging Face. With me to discuss this deal and what it might mean for the future of the AI sector for both companies and developers is our reporter Dan Robinson and senior reporter Tom Claburn. Thanks for coming on, guys. All right, so Dan, you broke this story, I think it was just yesterday now, and you were on the conference call that Nvidia and Hugging Face leadership held. So break it down for us. What was the agreement here? What's the deal? Well, like you said, it was a $12.9 billion purchase from Nvidia to acquire Hugging Face, which is the home of open AI, as I've been told. Open source and open weight. I just want to make sure it's not the home of OpenAI, the company, right? Yes, let's not get confused about that. They've entered into this agreement which is due to close next year, providing that they meet all the regulatory requirements, of course, and there are no objections from the authorities on that. It looks like Nvidia's agreed to keep a hands-off approach and allow Hugging Face to pretty much just do what they're already doing. But as we've been seeing, there are quite a few concerns around that, as Nvidia is a very powerful company in the AI market. And Hugging Face is the go-to place for open models. I'm sure most of our listeners know what Hugging Face is; it's essentially the GitHub for the AI space. It hosts models, and new open models tend to show up on Hugging Face first. There's also a pretty active community that discusses the development of these things on the platform. So this is obviously a pretty consequential move in this space and I know both Nvidia and Hugging Face framed it as giving Hugging Face more resources to pursue its mission. I think Clement, the CEO of Hugging Face, said that they want to reach a hundred million developers in the next few years and that they were worried that they wouldn't be able to without more resources. Let's be realistic. They're hosting a lot of very large AI models and all the data sets they contain. That's a lot of storage space and a lot of computing resources, not to mention serving and dealing with all the workloads that come with this. I imagine that could eat up a lot of cash and resources. Of course. And it's quite an ambitious goal given that they're at about 80 million users, I think it's estimated at the moment. And if you compare them with someone like GitHub, I think they are somewhere around 180 million since they've been taken over by Microsoft. And we've seen how well that's worked in the past few months with the stability issues and everything. It kind of begs the question too whether or not, if you get that big, can you remain stable and would Hugging Face end up suffering from some of the same problems that GitHub does? I wonder how long Hugging Face is going to continue to be flooded with new models because the models are appearing daily at Hugging Face. But what are the economics for making them? A lot of these companies are presumably not generating the revenue to pay for the cost of these models. And at some point, what are the economics of just giving away these models? Nvidia I think clearly sees that there's a future in the enterprise business where companies are going to want to control their own AI and run it. But is the pace of model development going to continue on into the future because where does all the money come from to pay for this? And it makes you wonder if this is part of the circular financing that we've seen throughout the industry. It raises a lot of questions. I was also surprised that one thing we haven't seen, which was talked about, was Nvidia purchasing a stake in Perplexity, which has been reported, but as far as I know hasn't actually happened. They were said to be looking around at investing some large sum in Perplexity. That hasn't been publicly announced yet, and I wonder if this deal changes that discussion. Perplexity, just remind me, do they make models? Well, they started off as AI search and are now kind of an AI agent platform. And then AMD maybe does something similar. It would make sense for them strategically as a defensive measure That feels like a net loss for the AI community, right? Hugging Face right now is kind of like the AI Switzerland; it's a platform that allows any and all comers to host their models on there, let people pick them apart, let people try them out. In the same way that a fracturing of GitHub would be an unfortunate loss, I feel like. You can argue that there's a centralized platform and that increases the chance of failure or loss of data. If everyone decides to go their own direction with this, I wonder what kind of net loss that would have for the AI development space. Everyone is going to be looking for a sort of edge, and whether you find that in terms of the model distribution or in the harness or somewhere else in the technology stack, the open weight models have become commodified very quickly. So you're either going to have the Anthropics and OpenAIs of the world if they can sustain their financial positions selling the high-end stuff to government clients, and then a bunch of quickly commoditized models that are going to only have differentiation based on what can be added to them, like if they work really well with a particular harness or if they really are well suited for Nvidia's hardware. Once you have that feedback loop between software and hardware, it makes switching harder, which is why the other companies have to respond. Huawei copies Samsung’s privacy display in its latest trifold
2026-09-07

Huawei copies Samsung’s privacy display in its latest trifold

The Verge | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
﻿The Mate XT 2 also borrows Samsung’s inward-folding design. ﻿The Mate XT 2 also borrows Samsung’s inward-folding design. Huawei has released its third trifold phone in China, and the company has clearly had half an eye on Samsung during development. Not only does the Mate XT 2 adopt the inward-folding form factor used on Samsung’s Galaxy Z TriFold, it’s also the first foldable to include a privacy-enhancing display, much like the one Samsung added to the Galaxy S26 Ultra this year. The first Mate XT, which launched in China in late 2024 and internationally a few months later, used an accordion-esque folding design. It removed the need to include an additional cover screen on the phone, but left parts of the delicate folding display permanently exposed. The follow-up XTs retained the design, but the XT 2 instead takes inspiration from Samsung’s trifold, folding both sides of the 10.2-inch flexible screen inwards to keep them safe from harm. More surprising is the addition of a privacy OLED display. This looks much like Samsung’s, so far only found on the S26 Ultra. When activated, it switches on pixels that emit light at narrower angles, making the screen appear almost black from the sides, but still visible head-on. It’s an optional extra, only available if you opt for 16GB of RAM and 1TB of storage and pay a further ¥1,000 (about $150) to add the privacy screen. Beyond the display, the XT 2 has Huawei’s new Kirin 9050 Pro chipset, improved water-resistance with an IP59 rating, and a triple rear camera. It launched today in China, starting at ¥19,999 ($3,000), though international release plans remain unknown. Samsung’s own Galaxy Z TriFold was discontinued shortly after its release last year, though leaks suggest that Samsung is still working on a follow-up. Seattle Times and Newsday sue OpenAI and Microsoft for infringement
2026-09-06

Seattle Times and Newsday sue OpenAI and Microsoft for infringement

The Verge | 2026-09-06

Visit Original Website
They want the AI models built on their works destroyed. They want the AI models built on their works destroyed. The Seattle Times and Newsday are just the latest plaintiffs to take OpenAI to court, alleging copyright infringement. The two outlets say the company used their journalism as training data for its AI models without permission and often reproduces passages from their reporting in response to user queries. This is similar to lawsuits filed by The New York Times, Ziff Davis, Merriam-Webster, and Encyclopedia Britannica against OpenAI. The Seattle Times and Newsday also named Microsoft as a defendant in the suit, since Copilot is built on OpenAI’s technology. The publishers join a list of nearly 400 local newspapers that recently sued the two companies, saying that because the chatbots reduce the need to visit their sites for reporting and answers, they cost them valuable subscription revenue. As part of the suit, The Seattle Times ​and Newsday are ⁠seeking the destruction of any copies of their works the companies are holding, as well as training datasets and AI models that incorporate them. OpenAI and Microsoft did not immediately respond to a request for comment. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. An Amazon cargo plane crashed at Miami International Airport
2026-09-06

An Amazon cargo plane crashed at Miami International Airport

The Verge | 2026-09-06

Visit Original Website
The Boeing 767-300 overran the runway during landing. The Boeing 767-300 overran the runway during landing. A plane bearing an Amazon logo overran the runway at Miami International Airport on Sunday during landing, crashing into vehicles and resulting in multiple injuries. The extent of the damage or the seriousness of the injuries was not clear at the time of publication. In the aftermath of the incident, the FAA released a statement saying: 21 Air Flight 7598 overran the runway after landing at Miami International Airport around 2 p.m. local time on Sunday, Sept. 6. The Boeing 767-300 cargo aircraft departed from Luis Muñoz Marín International Airport in San Juan, Puerto Rico. As a result, flights at the airport were grounded and over 60 Miami-Dade Fire Rescue units were deployed to the scene. When MDFR arrived, the plane was on fire and spewing heavy smoke, according to reports. Video from the scene posted by NBC 6 (below) clearly shows the Amazon logo on the plane’s tail and thick smoke rising from the scene where the plane came to a stop on the other side of the road from the airport. Amazon spokesperson Kelly Nantel told The Verge: “We can confirm that an Amazon Air plane operated by 21 Air experienced an incident while attempting to land at Miami International Airport today. This is a fast-moving situation and we’re still gathering details. We’re working closely with local authorities and officials to understand exactly what happened. Right now, our absolute priority is the safety, well-being, and care of everyone involved. We’re doing everything we can to support those affected.” Update September 6th: Added comment from Amazon. Europe has its first commercial orbital rocket
2026-09-06

Europe has its first commercial orbital rocket

The Verge | 2026-09-06

Visit Original Website
Isar Aerospace gives the continent more options to “buy European” for space launches. Isar Aerospace gives the continent more options to “buy European” for space launches. German company Isar Aerospace has successfully launched Europe’s first entirely commercial orbital rocket. It attempted to achieve the feat last March, but that lasted all of 30 seconds before the vehicle crashed into the sea and exploded. This time the company managed to get the two-stage Spectrum into low Earth orbit from a Norwegian spaceport. CEO and Co-Founder Daniel Metzler said in a press release: “Today, Isar Aerospace opened space from Continental Europe. Launch continues to be the largest bottleneck for the global space industry and from today on, there is a true alternative for commercial and institutional customers … Europe now has sovereign access to space. We have entered into a new chapter for European spaceflight.” Until now, Europe has been largely reliant on Arianespace and Avio, which are largely funded by governments. The current global political climate makes the accomplishment particularly important for European countries that might view the United States as a less reliable partner at the moment and that have been cut off from Russian Soyuz rockets by the country’s invasion of Ukraine. The European Space Agency has also made “buy European” a cornerstone of its policies. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Boox’s tiny Picco e-reader should land in November

The Verge | 2026-09-06

There won’t be magnets and there’s still no word on price, though. There won’t be magnets and there’s still no word on price, though. Boox teased the Picco, its take on the buzzy Xteink X4 e-reader, back in July, but provided almost no details. Now, thanks to some reporting out of IFA, we’ve got a bit more info, though sadly still no price. Unlike the Xteink devices, the Picco will not have magnets on the back for attaching to a phone. The 3.97-inch e-paper display, however, will be touchscreen, which The Verge’s Andrew Liszewski called a “welcome upgrade” on the X4 Pro. The Picco has physical page-turn buttons, and they can be customized with different actions for short and long presses. There’s apparently no onboard storage, and it relies entirely on a microSD card to hold books. But in addition to adding files to the card, you can transfer reading material to the Picco directly over USB-C (which the Xteink devices lack) or even WiFi from a companion app. The Picco won’t run Android, like Boox’s other devices, and will instead run a stripped-down, Linux-based OS. The company is including a handful of additional apps like a timer, a todo list, and a reading stats tracker. But, it’s unlikely that Boox will open its firmware like Xteink has, so it might be a less hackable and customizable device. There’s still some things we don’t know, like price, battery life, Bluetooth support, or if it will include a player for audiobooks. But all should be revealed in November, when it’s expected to launch. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. The Verge
The Fairphone 6 Plus is the midrange phone we desperately needed
2026-09-06

The Fairphone 6 Plus is the midrange phone we desperately needed

The Verge | 2026-09-06

Visit Original Website
﻿Fairphone’s proper US debut feels like a triumph in the middle of RAMageddon. If you buy something from a Verge link, Vox Media may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. If you buy something from a Verge link, Vox Media may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. The Fairphone 6 Plus feels like an extremely average midrange Android phone and I couldn’t be more thrilled. The mission has always been admirable. Fairphone seeks out ethically sourced materials and offers a high degree of repairability for its devices. But the phones themselves required a lot of sacrifices, like putting up with underpowered processors and bad cameras, in order to embrace the sustainability mission. The latest generations of Fairphone have inched toward closing that gap, and the 6 Plus achieves two huge milestones: It feels very much like using a middling Android phone, and it’s actually on sale in the US. The 6 Plus is a light update to last year’s Fairphone 6, which my colleague Dominic Preston reviewed and found very easy to recommend. The 6 Plus builds on it with a newer midrange Snapdragon chipset and a new color treatment. Fairphone says it’s cobalt; we know it around these parts as blurple. Otherwise, the phone is basically the same, and the replacement parts sold by Fairphone work interchangeably with both models. But above all, this phone is just fine. It runs about as fast as your typical mid-tier Razr or A-series Pixel. It offers a pleasantly close-to-stock version of Android free of bloat or gimmicky AI features. It’s just a phone. And that’s great. Highly repairableGreat warranty and software support lifespanSwappable back panels Dumbphone-esque focus modes are a nice touch Upfront cost is comparatively highNot as dust- and water-resistant as similarly priced phonesPower users might find battery life lacking I know, calling this a midrange phone when it’s priced at $649 might feel like a stretch. At best, it’s at the very top of the midrange class. But RAMageddon has done a real number on phone prices over the past year, and it’s not over yet. Given that the phone comes with 12GB of RAM, a five-year warranty, and up to six OS upgrades, that price feels more manageable. Also, you don’t need to add AppleCare or whatever to that price. You are the AppleCare. The 6 Plus has 12 user-replaceable parts including the battery, screen, USB-C port, and all three cameras. The repairs are extremely beginner-friendly — if you can use the tiny included screwdriver and follow some easy-to-parse directions, you won’t have a problem. The price of these parts compared to the cost of repairing a Google Pixel or Samsung Galaxy phone range from a pretty good deal to a hell of a good deal. Without an extended warranty of some kind, a battery replacement would typically cost in the ballpark of $100 to $180; Fairphone charges $39.99 for a new battery. A screen repair ranges from $100 to nearly $300; a new display from Fairphone costs $89.99. Add in the entirely reasonable cost of 30 minutes of your own labor (if that), and the long-term value of this phone starts looking pretty good. $649 is also a bargain compared to the $899 that the Fairphone 6 costs in the US. It was only offered preloaded with the privacy-minded /e/OS, which is a de-Googled version of Android made by a company called Murena. It’s not really for the faint of heart, and that price was pretty hard to swallow. But Fairphone is selling the 6 Plus directly in the US, with full support for T-Mobile and AT&T. I’ve had no trouble using it with my Verizon SIM for the past week, though it’s not certified on the network. Knowing how easy it is to replace parts of this phone as it ages actually required me to think a bit differently while I was using it. Battery life is just okay. I usually got through most days of light-to-moderate use with 60 percent left at bedtime. I did turn on every battery-draining feature I could think of, including the always-on display, higher screen refresh rate, and light mode. Those are all off by default, and probably for good reason. Normally I’d be pretty concerned about what that battery performance would look like in a few years. But there’s an easy fix: Just swap the battery yourself. No trip to the repair shop, no time away from your phone. Most phones would benefit from a new battery after a few years of continuous use anyway; at least with this one you can do it yourself and pay a lot less. The 6 Plus comes with way fewer compromises than early versions of the Fairphone did. The first iterations came with outdated designs, poor-quality cameras, and previous-gen displays. Things have steadily improved, but there are still some tradeoffs hanging around. The phone is rated IP55, which equates to mostly dust-resistant and mostly water-resistant, but not fully protected. Sealing up the phone completely and still offering easy repairability would be difficult, maybe impossible. That’s bad news, item one. Item two: no wireless charging, and likewise no ring of magnets a la Qi2 or MagSafe. I’m growing partial to an easily removable PopSocket on my phone, so I missed having that option here. Moving along: The screen is thoroughly decent, though it doesn’t get quite as bright in daylight as some of the nicest ones out there. The speakers are tinny. And even after more than a week with this phone, I still haven’t learned the muscle memory of finding the wake button / fingerprint scanner without looking, since it sits basically flush with the side of the phone. The Snapdragon 7S Gen 4 chipset handled the basic demands of modern life. If you poke around the edges you can find where this chipset falls short of the very best SoCs — which you can come by in devices that don’t cost much more than the Fairphone 6 Plus. Google Maps runs a little stuttery on my car’s infotainment screen when I run it with wired Android Auto, and there’s a bit of juddering as I scroll around my town in Pocket City 2. But it handles a dense webpage like Icy Veins’ character build guides remarkably well, and I can get away with a few back-to-back Portrait mode shots before the shutter button grays out to clear the buffer. Outside of very graphically intense gaming, it holds up just fine. Meeting that bar for a “decent midrange Android phone” means the Fairphone 6 Plus needs to offer a good, basic camera system. It does. It doesn’t offer one iota more than that, but it does the job. It was the camera I used to take pictures of my son’s fifth birthday, which in hindsight was a lot of trust to put into an unproven (by me at least) system. But it worked out! I have an in-focus photo of him blowing out birthday candles. That’s service journalism in action, folks. The main camera does a fine job with daylight scenes, and the app offers a 2x crop function that I used quite a bit. The ultrawide suffers some noise and smoothing in dimmer indoor light, which is pretty typical. Portrait mode is passable, and I didn’t notice any red flags in the handful of video clips I shot of my kid opening birthday presents. The camera system made some funky choices now and again, but overall I agree with Dom’s impressions of the same camera system on the preceding model: It’s fine. Not great, but certainly passable if you’re not picky. If that was all the Fairphone had to offer, I’d feel okay about recommending it. But there’s more, starting with the modular back panel accessories. The screwdriver included in the box is all you need to swap out the back plate and replace it with one that has a wallet or a flexible grip. There’s also a crossbody strap you can attach to any of those panels, though the jury’s still out on whether I personally can pull off a crossbody strap. This obviously isn’t as convenient as magnetic attachments, but it’s fun, and fun is hard to come by these days. The little neon-colored switch on the side of the phone is hard to miss, and it houses another one of the Fairphone’s bonus features. By default, switching it on enables something Fairphone calls “Moments,” which are basically focus modes with minimalist homescreens that limit you to a handful of apps. You can set up your own Moment modes and decide which apps you’ll have access to and who’s allowed to call you when the mode is enabled. I didn’t use this feature as much as I anticipated, mostly because I’m avoiding social media on my phone at the moment and that’s what I would most need a focus mode to keep me away from. But I think it’s a great concept and the execution appears to be sound. I’d appreciate a little more customizability, like maybe the ability to add a custom wallpaper rather than choosing from the basic options provided. But I guess the more options you add, the more room there is for distractions to wiggle in. Maybe basic really is best. Fairphone has finally created a phone that feels ready for the mainstream in the 6 and 6 Plus. It’s not just a device that represents some admirable ethical qualities — it’s a very good total package. There’s the feel-good benefit of buying from a company that, by every indication, walks the ethical and sustainability walk. But there are tangible benefits, too, like easily taking care of repairs yourself when you inevitably need them. And those upsides no longer come with such severe tradeoffs. When the price for a midrange phone seems to get higher every day, the 6 Plus starts to look like smart money.
